- **Code review (Medium) + security review, independently:** an in-code comment overstated the fix as closing the raw-error-text leak — it doesn't (see above). Corrected.
- **Code review (Low):** `reorderPoliciesMaxAttempts <= 0` would silently return `nil` (success) without the loop ever executing — not reachable today (positive compile-time constant) but a real footgun if that ever became configurable. Fixed: explicit guard returns an error instead.
- **Code review (Low) + security review (Low), independently:** the fixed 20ms backoff ignored `ctx` cancellation (a plain `time.Sleep`). Fixed: `select` on `ctx.Done()`/`time.After`.
- **Security review (Low):** the retry's correctness silently depends on `store.ReorderPolicies` being a bare autocommit statement (true today, per B-090's own design) — a future change wrapping it in an explicit transaction would make every retry attempt return a misleading `25P02` instead of the real `40P01`. Documented explicitly in `reorderPoliciesWithRetry`'s doc comment as a "do not call with a tx-scoped Queries" constraint, not fixed structurally (no transaction exists to guard against today).
- **Code review (Low, test quality):** one of the two new real-Postgres tests simulated a deadlock on every attempt including the last, so no attempt ever reached real Postgres and its "no partial state" assertion was vacuously true — the review caught that nothing could have corrupted state that was never touched. Rewritten: 2 simulated deadlocks + a REAL final attempt against real Postgres that hits a genuine final-state priority collision (mirroring `TestReorderPolicies_RealDB_GenuineCollision_RollsBackCleanly`'s exact shape) — now a real, non-vacuous proof that exhausting the deadlock budget correctly falls through to a different real terminal failure with zero corruption.
- **Security review, adversarially traced, all clean (see BUILT.md for full detail):** retry inputs (`uc.OrgID`, `req.Order`) are byte-identical on every attempt, never mutated; `reorderPoliciesExecFunc` has zero runtime/request-reachable attack surface (compile-time test seam only, confirmed via repo-wide grep); the 20ms×2 backoff introduces no meaningful new DoS surface (no connection/lock held during sleep); `40P01` is narrow enough to not mask other errors; B-090's duplicate-ID rejection and org-scoping are both unaffected (the retry wrapper sits strictly inside the existing `s.queries != nil` block, after both checks).
- **Security review (Medium), a real PRE-EXISTING production gap found while looking for B-115's production analogue, not fixed here (outside this brief's file scope):** `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/dispatcher.go` silently discards `auditWriter.Write` errors on the `denied`/`escalated` branches (lines ~313, ~328, ~437 — `_ = d.auditWriter.Write(...)`), while the `allowed` branch does log a failure (`slog.Error`). Under sustained DB write pressure, a policy denial or human-approval escalation — the two most governance-relevant outcomes — could leave zero audit trace with no signal anywhere, while the hash chain stays internally consistent over the surviving rows (undetectable after the fact). Logged fresh as **B-121** below, flagged as the highest-priority of this session's 3 new findings — a real reliability gap in the audit trail on the security-critical path, not test-only.
**Test coverage:** `policies_reorder_retry_test.go` (new, internal `package api`) — 4 pure unit tests for `isReorderDeadlock`, 3 pure control-flow tests for `reorderPoliciesWithRetry` (persistent-deadlock-exhausts, non-deadlock-not-retried, success-on-first-attempt), 2 real-Postgres tests (simulated-deadlock-then-real-success; 2 simulated deadlocks then a real terminal collision, proving no partial state), 1 constants sanity check. B-090's own `TestReorderPolicies_RealDB_ConcurrentOverlappingReorders_NoCorruption` re-run byte-identical (calls `q.ReorderPolicies` directly, never the handler or retry wrapper) — confirmed unaffected. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test -count=1 ./...` clean across the entire `eami-api` module against a real Postgres, zero regressions.
**Live-verified against the real rebuilt/restarted `eami-api` container:** a real admin login, 5 real policies created via the real HTTP API, then 30 iterations of 2 genuinely concurrent overlapping `POST /v1/gateway/policies/reorder` HTTP requests (60 real requests total, scaled up from B-090's own 20-iteration precedent) — zero non-200 responses, final DB state confirmed a valid, uncorrupted permutation via direct `psql`. All fixtures cleaned up, confirmed 0 remaining.
**Dependencies:** B-090 (found this while reviewing). New discovered work: **B-121** (dispatcher.go audit-write-error swallowing, Medium-High priority per explicit user direction).

### B-118 — `POST /v1/gateway/tokens` has no rate limiting; B-107's fire-and-forget audit write means a stalled DB accumulates unbounded goroutines under load — **DONE, 2026-08-27**
**Objective:** found by the mandatory code-review pass on B-090/107 — unlike the workflow route (`cmd/gateway/main.go`), `/v1/gateway/tokens` has no rate limiting. With B-107's `RecordIssued` now async, one authenticated client can pipeline issuances and queue goroutines faster than they drain, each holding a pgxpool connection for up to the new 5s bounded timeout — the same pool serving policy loading, registry lookups, and dispatch-path audit writes. Pre-existing gap (no rate limiting existed before B-107 either), but B-107's change makes sustained concurrency cheaper for a caller to generate (no longer bounded by synchronous-write latency).
**Severity going in:** Low-Medium.
**What shipped:** a small in-memory per-agent fixed-window(-log) rate limiter (`rateLimiter`/`newRateLimiter`/`Allow`/`setRetryAfter` in `issue_http.go`), algorithm deliberately duplicated — not imported — from `eami-gateway/internal/workflow/ratelimit.go`'s B-070 precedent (mutex-protected `map[string][]time.Time`, fail-closed on non-positive limit, `Retry-After` rounded up per RFC 9110 §10.2.3). Hardcoded at **20 requests / 60s per resolved agent UUID** (`tokenIssueRateLimit`/`tokenIssueRateLimitWindow` constants), constructed once in `NewIssueHandler`. **Deliberately not env-configurable like B-070's `WorkflowRunPerAgent`** — confirmed with the user before building: closing "no rate limiting exists at all" mattered more than operator-tunability, and env-configurability would have required touching `config.go`/`main.go`, outside this brief's granted `apikey.go`/`issue_http.go` scope. Full configurability logged fresh as **B-119** (below), not built here. Checked *after* API-key validation and B-098's cross-agent scoping succeed (same ordering precedent as `workflow/ratelimit.go`'s `RateLimitRunMiddleware` — an invalid/misscoped request never consumes a legitimate agent's quota), keyed on `rec.ID` (the resolved agent's real registry UUID, not the client-claimed name — same cross-org-collision avoidance as B-042/B-070).
**A real gap surfaced by both mandatory review passes independently (code review AND security review), disclosed not fixed here:** the endpoint remains entirely unprotected on the *pre-auth* path — a flood of requests with bogus `X-API-Key` values still each cost a real indexed Postgres lookup (`ValidateAndResolveAgent`'s query) against the same shared pgxpool serving MCP dispatch, policy loading, and workflow runs, before ever reaching the per-agent limiter (which by design only sees already-authenticated, correctly-scoped callers). Correctly out of *this* fix's scope (B-118's own objective was specifically "one **authenticated** client... pipelines issuances") but a real, adjacent capacity concern on an internet-reachable route (`docker/caddy` fronts `:8443` straight to the gateway with no rate-limiting layer of its own) — logged fresh as **B-120** (below), not built here.
**Also disclosed, not fixed (both accepted/low, inherited from B-070's own precedent, not regressions):** the `attempts` map has no eviction (bounded by real agent count, ≤20 `time.Time` per key, same property `workflow/ratelimit.go` already has); the limiter is actually a sliding-window log, not a true fixed window (stricter, not weaker — no boundary double-burst — comment inherited "fixed-window" naming from B-070 for consistency, technically imprecise).
**Test coverage:** `issue_ratelimit_test.go` — 7 pure-unit tests needing no Postgres (boundary at exactly the limit, per-key bucket isolation, **window-actually-expires-and-recovers** with a short test-only window, denied attempts don't extend the window, non-positive-limit fail-closed, `Retry-After` rounding) — added specifically because the hardcoded 20/60s constants meant the integration tests alone couldn't cheaply prove the window ever expires. `issue_http_b116_b118_pg_test.go` (real Postgres) proves the live 429 trip with a real `Retry-After` header and per-agent bucket isolation using the real 20-request production limit. Live-verified against the real rebuilt/restarted gateway container: 20 real successful issuances, a real 429 with `Retry-After: 36` on the 21st, cross-agent scoping still 403, a second agent's own bucket confirmed unaffected, and `ai_token_events` confirmed to hold exactly 20 `issued` rows (not 21 — the rate-limited request never reached `Manager.Issue`). All fixtures cleaned up, confirmed 0 remaining.
**Dependencies:** B-107 (the change that made this more reachable), B-116 (built together, same brief).

### B-119 — Full env-configurable rate limiting for `POST /v1/gateway/tokens`, matching B-070's `WorkflowRunPerAgent` pattern
**Objective:** found while building B-118 (this session, 2026-08-27) — B-118 shipped a hardcoded 20-request/60s per-agent limiter (constants in `issue_http.go`) rather than an env-configurable one like B-070's `WorkflowRunPerAgent`/`WorkflowRunPerAgentWindowSeconds`, specifically to stay inside this brief's granted file scope (`apikey.go`/`issue_http.go` only — no `config.go`/`main.go`). An operator cannot currently tune the threshold without a code change and redeploy.
**Severity:** Low — the gap this closes (no rate limiting at all) is already closed; this is pure operability/tunability, not a security gap.
**Fix sketch:** add `RateLimitConfig` fields (e.g. `TokenIssuePerAgent`/`TokenIssuePerAgentWindowSeconds`) to `eami-gateway/internal/config/config.go` mirroring the existing `WorkflowRunPerAgent` fields exactly (env vars, defaults, non-negative validation), thread them into `NewIssueHandler`'s construction in `cmd/gateway/main.go`, replacing the `tokenIssueRateLimit`/`tokenIssueRateLimitWindow` constants.
**Dependencies:** B-118 (the mechanism this makes tunable), B-070 (the pattern to mirror).

### B-120 — `POST /v1/gateway/tokens` has no PRE-authentication rate limiting; an unauthenticated flood still costs a real DB round trip against the shared pool
**Objective:** found by this brief's own mandatory code-review AND security-review passes independently (2026-08-27), while building B-118 — B-118's new per-agent limiter is checked only after API-key validation and cross-agent scoping succeed (by design, so an invalid/misscoped caller can't burn a real agent's quota), which means a request with a bogus or garbage `X-API-Key` never reaches the limiter at all, while still executing `ValidateAndResolveAgent`'s real indexed Postgres query (`key_hash` lookup + `gateway_agents` LEFT JOIN) against `eami-gateway`'s single shared `pgxpool` — the same pool serving MCP tool-call dispatch, policy loading, workflow runs, and audit writes. `docker/caddy/docker-entrypoint.sh` fronts the gateway's port directly with no rate-limiting layer of its own, so this route is internet-reachable in the production topology. Pre-existing gap, not introduced by B-118 — B-118 only closed the *authenticated* half of the threat model its own backlog text named.
**Severity:** Medium-Low — the per-request cost is cheap (one indexed lookup, ~8KiB body cap already in place since B-090/107), but a sustained flood competes for pool connections/acquisition-queue slots with every other gateway function, on a security-enforcement-path service.
**Fix sketch:** a second, coarser limiter keyed pre-auth (source IP, or a cheap hash of the raw presented key) checked before `ValidateAndResolveAgent` is called — or a bounded-concurrency guard in front of the key lookup. Needs its own design pass (which key to use, what threshold) rather than a copy-paste of B-118's per-agent limiter, since the threat model (anonymous flood) is different from B-118's (authenticated pipelining).
**Dependencies:** B-118 (the fix this is adjacent to, built same session).

### B-121 — `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/dispatcher.go` silently discards `audit_log` write errors on the `denied`/`escalated` branches (unlike `allowed`, which logs) — **DONE, 2026-08-27**
**Objective:** found by the mandatory security-review pass on B-115/B-117, while looking for a production analogue of B-115's discarded-audit-write-error pattern. Real production code, not test-only: `dispatcher.go`'s `denied` branch, `escalated` branch, and the `allowed`-branch-with-proxy-error case all did `_ = d.auditWriter.Write(reqCtx, auditEntry)`, silently swallowing any write failure — while the genuinely-successful `allowed` branch did `if writeErr := ...; writeErr != nil { slog.Error(...) }`. The inconsistency ran backwards: the two most governance-relevant outcomes (a policy denial, a human-approval escalation) were the ones written blind.
**Severity going in:** Medium-High, per explicit user direction (highest priority of this session's 3 new findings at the time) — a real reliability gap in the audit trail's completeness on the security-critical path.
**Investigated before building, per the brief's own explicit ask:** does the fix genuinely fit B-102's hook mechanism? Partially — the hook loop runs only AFTER an outcome is fully known, uniformly timed, but `Dispatch`'s own doc comment establishes audit-write TIMING is deliberately branch-specific (Escalate writes before blocking on `Hold()`) and predates B-102; moving the `Write()` call itself into a hook would have changed that. What genuinely fits the hook mechanism is the *error handling*, not the write's timing.
**What shipped:** `DispatchOutcome` gained `AuditWriteErr error` (the error from that branch's own `Write()` call, captured at its existing call site and carried through — both of Escalate's two exit points share the one write before `Hold()`, so both propagate the same value) and `AuditDecision string` (see the security-review finding below). One new hook, `d.logAuditWriteFailureHook` — registered FIRST in the hooks list (code-review finding, below) — logs at Error level when `AuditWriteErr != nil`; no-op otherwise. Allow-success's old inline `if writeErr != nil { slog.Error(...) }` was removed — it now flows through the same shared hook as the other 3 sites, so all 4 are handled identically by construction, not "3 fixed + 1 pre-existing special case." **Logging, not a stronger alert/metric, is the deliberate choice** (confirmed with the user before building): `eami-gateway` has no existing metrics/alerting infrastructure to escalate into (no Prometheus, no expvar; `eami-api`'s `internal/alerting` engine is a separate module for business-metric rules, not reachable from here and not a fit for an in-process DB-write signal).
**Mandatory reviewer + security passes, both ran, both found real issues, all fixed:**
- **Security review (Medium), the most severe finding:** the Allow-proxy-failure branch sets `DispatchOutcome.Decision="allowed"` (the policy-branch label) but writes `auditEntry.Decision="denied"` to `audit_log` — the first draft's log line reused `Decision`, so a lost row from this branch would have told an operator to look for a "denied" gap filed under "allowed," exactly backwards. Fixed with the new `AuditDecision` field, set to the literal value passed to `Write` at all 4 sites, so the log is trustworthy by construction rather than 3-of-4 branches happening to agree. Live-verified: a real dispatch to an unregistered tool with no downstream configured hit exactly this branch, and the real `audit_log` row's `decision` column was confirmed `"denied"` via direct `psql`, confirming the fix's premise against real production behavior, not just the test's assumption.
- **Code review (Medium):** the new log line only included `org_id`/`agent`/`tool`/`action`/`decision`/`err` — insufficient to correlate a lost row against `workflow_run_steps` (no FK to/from `audit_log` at all, per `ActionContext`'s own doc comment) or the approval subsystem. Fixed: added `agent_uuid`/`session_id`/`workflow_run_id`/`step_index`.
- **Code review (Low-Medium):** the new hook was registered last, behind two hooks with no panic recovery in the loop — a panic in an earlier hook would have silently prevented the audit-integrity signal from ever firing. Fixed: `logAuditWriteFailureHook` registered first; it has zero dependency on the other two.
- **A real bug found mid-session by the session itself, not either review pass, while implementing the code-review's "don't silently swallow other log output" test-quality fix:** an early attempt to have the test capture handler forward to the real previous default handler deadlocked — Go's `slog.defaultHandler` (installed when nothing has customized `slog.Default()`) re-enters the classic `log` package's non-reentrant mutex when called from inside another handler's `Handle`. Confirmed via a real hang and `go test -timeout` panic showing two goroutines both blocked on the identical mutex, one nested in the other's call stack. Reverted to a non-forwarding capture handler that dumps captured records via `t.Logf` only on test failure instead — same debuggability, no live handler-chain re-entrancy.
- **Code review (Low), test quality:** a `captureHandler.WithAttrs`/`WithGroup` correctness gap and an incomplete `t.Parallel()` safety comment — both fixed/corrected (documented as intentionally-unimplemented and corrected respectively, since a full attrs-merge implementation would be untested, unused complexity).
**Disclosed, not fixed here, all confirmed with the user before minting B-IDs (precedent from this session's own prior batches — no explicit response received before this doc update; proceeding per that established precedent, flagged for the user to redirect if they'd rather combine any of these):**
- **B-124** (Medium-High) — an approved escalation's actual downstream execution writes no `audit_log` row at all (only the pre-`Hold()` "escalated" row exists) — a real gap in the audit trail's core claim, found while confirming B-121's fix covered every `Write` call site (there are only 4, all in `dispatcher.go`, confirmed by repo-wide grep — the reason there's no 5th site to fix is that the post-approval execution genuinely never calls `audit.Writer` at all).
- **B-125** (Medium) — `audit_log.approval_id`/`approved_by` are never populated by the gateway anywhere, despite `eami-api`'s audit API reading both back into its response — related to B-124 but a distinct fix (populating these on the existing "escalated" row vs. writing a whole new post-execution row).
- **B-126** (Low, test-only) — `internal/workflow/testenv_test.go`'s reconstructed dispatch closure still discards audit-write errors identically to B-121's original bug — zero production impact (production routes through the real, now-fixed `Dispatcher.Dispatch` via `mcp.DecisionHandler`), confirmed by checking as the brief's own fix sketch asked, not fixed (out of this brief's `MAY MODIFY: dispatcher.go`-only scope).
- **B-127** (Low, test quality, pre-existing not introduced here) — `recordEpisodeHook`'s detached `go Record(...)` goroutine outlives its own test and loses its race against `newMainTestEnv`'s org-delete cleanup (visible as a recurring `episodes_org_id_fkey` warning across this session's test runs) — every episode assertion in `dispatcher_test.go`/`testenv_test.go` is silently testing a write that never lands.
**Test coverage:** `dispatcher_test.go` gained a `failingAuditDB` (implements `audit.WriterDB`, the package's own documented test seam) for deterministic failure injection, plus 8 new tests: Deny/Escalate-Submit-failure/Escalate-resumed/Allow all logged correctly (AC1-AC3), a negative-case (successful write logs nothing), the AC4-style "one hook, three decision types, one production change" proof (mirroring B-102's own `TestDispatch_NewHook_FiresForAllThreeDecisionTypes`), and the `AuditDecision`-mismatch regression test (a real forced proxy failure via a closed listener, not a simulated one). **Verified 2026-08-27: `go build`/`go vet`/`go test -count=1 -timeout 180s ./...` clean across the entire `eami-gateway` module** against a real Postgres, zero regressions.
**Live-verified against the real rebuilt/restarted gateway container:** a real seeded org/agent/API key/deny+allow policies, a real agent JWT, a real SSE session, and two real `tool_call` dispatches through the actual running gateway (both landed on the Allow-proxy-failure branch — no downstream is configured in this dev topology — which happens to be exactly the branch the `AuditDecision` bug lived in). Confirmed via direct `psql`: both real `audit_log` rows have `decision="denied"`, matching the fix's premise. Confirmed via `docker compose logs`: zero `"dispatch: audit_log write failed"` lines under this normal, fully-successful run (no false positives). Per the same principle the user set for B-115/B-117's live verification (deterministic fake-DB tests are the proof for the failure-injection scenarios; live verification confirms normal behavior is unaffected), a real audit-write failure was not forced against the live stack. Fixtures cleaned up; the agent was suspended rather than hard-deleted (real episode history from the live dispatch, same `DeleteAgent` audit-trail guard precedent as prior sessions).
**Dependencies:** none. Found by, not blocking, B-115/B-117. New discovered work: **B-124**, **B-125**, **B-126**, **B-127**.

### B-124/B-125 — An approved escalation's actual downstream execution writes no `audit_log` row at all, and `approval_id`/`approved_by` are never populated — **DONE, 2026-08-28**
**Objective:** found by the mandatory code-review pass on B-121 while confirming B-121's fix covered every `d.auditWriter.Write` call site — a repo-wide grep confirmed there are exactly 4, all in `dispatcher.go`'s `Escalate` (pre-`Hold()`) and `Deny`/`Allow` branches. There is no 5th site because the Escalate branch's real downstream execution, which happens AFTER `Hold()` resumes a human-approved call, never calls `audit.Writer` at all. So an approved-and-executed escalation was recorded only by the "escalated" row written before the human ever decided; whatever actually happened after approval (success, failure, a different result than the approver reviewed) left no trace in the tamper-evident audit log — and `audit_log.approval_id`/`approved_by`, real columns `Writer` already inserts and `eami-api`'s audit endpoint already reads back, had nothing anywhere in `eami-gateway` ever assigning them.
**Severity:** Medium-High — per explicit user direction, "arguably more consequential than B-121 itself, since it means the record of what actually happened after approval may not exist at all." Scoped together per explicit user direction (same code path: a post-approval-resume write naturally has both the execution outcome and the approver identity in scope at the same call site).
**Critical prerequisite investigated and completed first, per the brief's own explicit ask:** `approval.Router.Hold()` had 4 independent exit paths (normal resolve() via channel, race-window timeout backstop, genuine timeout, ctx-cancelled) but only 2 converged on the shared `outcomeFromStatus` construction point — adding a new audit hook without first converging these exits would have silently reproduced B-099's exact bug shape (a return point nothing structurally forces through the shared step) in a new location. Converged first, proven by its own dedicated test (`TestHold_CtxCancelled_NotResolved`, the AC5 "one hook fires for all paths" proof), before building the actual fix.
**What shipped:** `Hold()`'s return type changed from `(json.RawMessage, error)` to a new exported `HoldOutcome` struct (`Result`, `Err`, `Resolved`, `Status`, `ApprovedBy`, `DecisionReason`) — a deliberate, contained signature change (7 call sites, 1 production). All 3 resolving paths (normal resolve, timeout-window race backstop, genuine timeout) now converge on one `outcomeFromStatus` call; the genuine-timeout path's error text changed from `"approval timed out after %s"` to route through the same shared construction (confirmed via grep nothing depended on the old string, per explicit user approval). `outcomeFromStatus`'s default case hardened: `Resolved` is only ever true for `"denied"`/`"expired"` status values, guarding against a future unrecognized `approval_requests.status` value silently writing a wrong row into the immutable chain. `DispatchOutcome` gained `ResolutionAuditEntry *audit.Entry`, built once in `Dispatch()` itself (not inside a hook — the fields it needs, like already-masked `Parameters`/`DataHandling`/`PolicyID`, only exist as `Dispatch()`-local state). A new hook, `recordEscalationResolutionAuditHook`, wrapped in `safego.Guard`, writes it via `context.WithoutCancel(ctx)` so the write survives `net/http`'s auto-cancellation on handler return. Symmetric across approved/denied/expired (per the brief's explicit assumption); ctx-cancelled deliberately writes nothing (no decision was ever made; the original "escalated" row stands as the complete record). Uses `approval_id IS NOT NULL` as the distinguishing mechanism versus a new decision-enum value, avoiding a migration.
**The attribution fix (found independently by both mandatory review passes, and the single most-exercised piece of logic in live verification below):** pairing `Decision="denied"` with the real approver's identity would misleadingly read as "this person denied it" for a case where the approver actually said yes but the resumed dispatch itself failed (e.g. a TOCTOU rejection, or — as live verification below hit directly — no downstream configured). Fixed with a conditional: `ApprovedBy` is only copied onto the resolution row when `Decision=="allowed"` OR the human's own decision wasn't `"approved"` (a genuine denial) — never for the "approved but execution failed" case, where the row correctly shows `decision="denied"`, a real `approval_id`, and an empty `approved_by`.
**Mandatory reviewer + security passes, both ran, both found real issues, all fixed:** the attribution fix above (independently found by both); a cross-module invariant (the genuine-timeout path's "no once-guard needed" reasoning depends on `eami-api`'s `DecideApproval` UPDATE having `WHERE id=$1 AND status='pending'`) strengthened from implicit to explicitly named in a comment; a silent-error-swallowing bug in `resolveHoldTimeout`'s deepest fallback branch (reassigned `err` to the real fetch error instead of losing it) fixed; `router_holdconvergence_test.go`'s naming/placement and `dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go`'s `verifyHashChain` helper both hardened per findings below.
**A real test-flakiness bug the session found in its own test code, not either review pass:** an earlier draft of `verifyHashChain` snapshotted the audit chain's head *before* `Dispatch()` ran and asserted the first new row's `prev_hash` matched exactly — flaky under `go test ./...`'s concurrent package execution (`audit_log` is one global chain; another package's test could legitimately write a row in the gap). Fixed: trusts the test's own first row's stored `prev_hash` as the starting point, verifies internal consistency of only the test's own rows, and (strengthened further per a code-review finding) confirms that stored `prev_hash` genuinely corresponds to a real existing row's hash or the genesis hash — closing the "chain was reset, not continued" blind spot without reintroducing the race.
**Disclosed, not fixed here, confirmed with the user before minting:** the hash chain formula itself doesn't cover `approval_id`/`approved_by` (Medium-High, out of scope per the brief's explicit MUST NOT MODIFY on the hash-chain formula) — a future tamper of either field on a resolution row wouldn't be caught by hash verification, only by the fact the row exists at all; `DecisionReason` has no schema column to land on (Low). Both need their own scoped follow-up, not minted here without founder confirmation.
**Test coverage:** 14+ real-Postgres integration tests across `internal/approval` and `cmd/gateway`, covering all 7 acceptance criteria (approved/denied/expired → 2nd row, hash-verified; ctx-cancelled → no 2nd row; the Hold() convergence proof itself; full hash-chain re-verification across a mixed sequence; zero regression to B-121/B-100/normal dispatch) — run clean 4+ times consecutively, including under `go test ./...`'s full concurrent-package conditions. **Verified 2026-08-28: `go build`/`go vet`/`go test -count=1 ./...` clean across `eami-gateway`.**
**Live-verified against the real rebuilt gateway container (this pass required B-129's fix to be usable at all — see that entry; a real escalate policy could not be reliably exercised live before B-129 landed):** a fresh org/admin/agent/API key seeded, an escalate policy created and live-reloaded with zero restart, two real escalations dispatched through the real running gateway and decided via the real `POST /v1/approvals/{id}/decide` endpoint. **The approved path live-exercised the attribution fix's exact edge case** (no downstream configured in this dev topology, same limitation as B-121's own live verification): the human approved, the resumed dispatch failed at the proxy, and the real resolution row correctly showed `decision="denied"`, a real `approval_id`, and `approved_by` left empty — proving the attribution fix by construction, not assumption. **The denied path live-exercised the normal case cleanly:** a genuine human denial produced a resolution row with `decision="denied"`, the real `approval_id`, and `approved_by` correctly populated with the real approver's user ID. All 4 real audit_log rows across both escalations (2 "escalated" + 2 resolution) were independently re-hashed byte-for-byte outside the application (a standalone bash/sha256sum reimplementation of the documented `SHA-256(prevHash||id||orgID||agentName||toolName||action||decision||timestamp)` formula) and matched the stored `hash` column exactly for all 4 rows — a genuine external tamper-evidence proof, not a trust of the stored `prev_hash`/`hash` linkage. Zero `"escalation-resolution-audit-write"` failure log lines under this normal run (no false positives). The expired path and ctx-cancelled path were not live-exercised (10-minute default hold timeout makes a live expiry impractical; ctx-cancellation isn't observable through the HTTP surface) — both remain proven only by the real-Postgres Go integration tests above, consistent with this session's established standard (deterministic tests prove failure-injection/unreachable-live scenarios; live verification confirms the normal, reachable paths). All fixtures cleaned up, confirmed 0 remaining via direct `psql`.
**Dependencies:** B-121 (found while verifying its own coverage). **Blocked, then unblocked, by:** B-129 (policy hot-reload fix — without it, no escalate policy created after gateway startup could ever be exercised live; found and fixed as its own urgent, separate brief during this one's live-verification step). **Related, logged separately, not fixed:** B-128 (no org scoping in policy evaluation — pre-existing, unrelated to this fix's own correctness, disclosed as an open environmental gap the live-verification org/policy exists inside of).
**Dependencies:** B-121 (found while verifying its own coverage). Related: B-124 (likely same fix or closely coupled).

### B-126 — `internal/workflow/testenv_test.go`'s reconstructed dispatch closure still discards audit-write errors, identically to B-121's original bug
**Objective:** found by directly checking, as B-121's own fix sketch explicitly asked ("check whether its own reconstructed dispatch logic (testenv_test.go) has the identical asymmetry and needs the same fix for consistency, even though that file is test-only") — confirmed via grep: all 4 `_ = auditWriter.Write(ctx, auditEntry)` discard sites are still present, unchanged, in `testenv_test.go`'s hand-reconstructed dispatch shape (needed because `package main` can't be imported by another package's tests — see that file's own header). Zero production impact: `internal/workflow/executor.go`'s real `Executor.dispatch` field is `mcp.DecisionHandler`, the SAME `Dispatcher.Dispatch` method B-121 fixed, wired from `cmd/gateway/main.go` — confirmed by reading `executor.go` directly, not assumed.
**Severity:** Low — test-only, and even within tests, nothing currently asserts on `internal/workflow` package tests' audit-write-failure behavior (unlike `dispatcher_test.go`, which now does, for the real production code).
**Fix sketch:** mirror B-121's `AuditWriteErr`-capture-and-log pattern in `testenv_test.go`'s reconstructed closure, purely so a future `internal/workflow` test could exercise this scenario the same way `dispatcher_test.go` now can — low urgency, since no such test currently exists or is planned.
**Dependencies:** B-121 (the pattern to mirror). Out of that brief's own scope (`MAY MODIFY: dispatcher.go` only), confirmed-not-fixed rather than silently left unchecked.

### B-127 — Pre-existing test race: `recordEpisodeHook`'s detached goroutine outlives its own test, silently testing a write that never lands
**Objective:** found by the mandatory code-review pass on B-121, empirically confirmed via repeated `-v` test output across this session — every `dispatcher_test.go`/`internal/workflow` test that exercises a branch with `EpisodeSteps` set spawns `go d.episodeRecorder.Record(context.Background(), ...)` (by design, B-027 — `Record` doesn't spawn its own goroutine, the caller must), which regularly outlives the test function itself and loses its race against `newMainTestEnv`'s `t.Cleanup`-based `DELETE FROM orgs` — visible as a recurring `WARN episode: db write failed ... episodes_org_id_fkey` in test output. Every episode-related assertion in these test files has therefore been silently testing a write that (per the FK violation) never actually lands.
**Severity:** Low — test-quality only, not a production bug (`context.Background()` for episode recording is itself deliberate and correct in production, where nothing analogous to `t.Cleanup`'s org-delete race exists) and not introduced by B-121 (pre-existing across many sessions' worth of tests in this file).
**Fix sketch:** needs investigation, not a reflexive fix — options include awaiting the episode goroutine before a test's own cleanup fires (a test-only synchronization point, e.g. a channel/WaitGroup passed through a test-only hook), or accepting the race as a known limitation and documenting that these tests only prove the goroutine was SPAWNED correctly, not that its write succeeds. Whoever picks this up should decide which before touching test code broadly.
**Dependencies:** B-121 (the session that surfaced it via repeated live test-output review). Affects `dispatcher_test.go` and `internal/workflow`'s test files.

### B-122 — B-115's fix now leaves permanent orphaned `audit_log` rows in the shared dev database on every `eami-gateway/internal/workflow` test run
**Objective:** found live by the mandatory code-review pass on B-115/B-117 — before B-115, every allow-path `audit_log` INSERT from a test using `allowAllRules()` failed silently (the root bug B-115 fixed). After the fix, those INSERTs now succeed for real — but `audit_log` has no FK to `orgs` (deliberate, append-only design; confirmed via schema), so `newWorkflowTestEnv`'s `t.Cleanup`-based `DELETE FROM orgs` never cascades to the audit rows those tests wrote. Measured live during this session: a single `go test ./internal/workflow/...` run took matching rows (`agent_name LIKE 'wf-exec-agent-%'`) from 43 to 59 — all 59 orphaned (no matching org). This will keep growing, unbounded, on every future test run against the shared dev database.
**Severity:** Medium-Low — not a security issue and not corruption (the hash chain stays valid; this is authentic append-only test data, not garbage), but a real, growing operational annoyance in the shared dev DB that will eventually affect anyone querying `audit_log` for real diagnostic purposes in that environment.
**Fix sketch:** needs a real design decision, not a reflexive cleanup — deleting mid-chain rows breaks `verify-audit-log.sh`'s hash-chain verification (confirmed: this table is `audit.Writer`'s tamper-evident hash chain, per its own package doc). Two real options to weigh: (a) point `internal/workflow`'s real-Postgres tests at an isolated/ephemeral test database instead of the shared dev one (larger infra change, more correct long-term); (b) accept the growth as a known, documented property of running these specific tests against the shared dev DB (cheaper, but the pollution is unbounded over time). Whoever picks this up should decide before touching any code.
**Dependencies:** B-115 (the fix whose correctness caused this side effect — not a defect in that fix itself).
**Supporting evidence (2026-08-29): B-122's risk manifested as a real CI failure, not just theoretical residue.** CI run 33202211503 (job 98954194411, commit `f510f17`, a docs-only change) failed `TestDispatch_Escalate_Resolution_Expired_WritesSecondRow` in `cmd/gateway` with a hash-chain continuity break (`dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go:310`: `rows[0]` claimed a `prev_hash` that matched no row in `audit_log` and wasn't the genesis hash — "the chain was reset, not continued"), immediately preceded by a WARN showing a severed TLS/SASL DB connection mid-test. Investigated per explicit user direction rather than reflexively filed as a new bug: confirmed the run postdates the B-115 UUID fix (not stale), then re-ran the full `eami-gateway` suite against a fresh, CI-matching Postgres (`timescale/timescaledb-ha:pg16`, real `migrate` CLI schema apply, same `TEST_DATABASE_URL` shape as CI) — 3 full `go test ./... -count=1` runs (all green) plus 20 targeted repetitions of the specific failing test and its 3 siblings (20/20 pass), zero reproduction. Confirmed as a genuine environmental flake, not a reproducible logic bug in the resolution-audit-write path (B-124/B-125) — but concrete evidence that the shared-audit_log cross-test interference risk this entry already flagged can and does surface as visible CI failures, not just accumulating residue. No new B-ID minted at the time; factor into this entry's eventual priority.
**Update (2026-08-29): the same test failed again in CI, promoted to its own item.** `TestDispatch_Escalate_Resolution_Expired_WritesSecondRow` recurred with an identical failure signature (same file/line, same "chain was reset, not continued" shape) via a different proximate trigger (no severed connection this time; an `episodes_org_id_fkey` violation appeared instead, after the failure). Two occurrences with the same symptom via different triggers reads as a real timing-sensitive race, not a one-off — promoted to **B-140** for its own investigation rather than continuing to log as passive supporting evidence here. This entry (B-122) remains the correct place for the specific orphaned-row/no-cleanup-mechanism finding; B-140 is the correct place for the broader race-condition investigation this recurrence motivated. See B-140.
**Resolution (2026-08-29): B-140's investigation resolved this entry's own open fork — the fix sketch's option (a) is now the confirmed right call, backed by real evidence, not just the "more correct long-term" cost/benefit guess this entry originally offered.** B-140 traced the exact mechanism behind its own CI recurrences to the same root cause this entry already named: `eami-gateway`'s real-Postgres tests share one global `audit_log`/`orgs` state with no per-test isolation. Confirmed via direct code reading: adopting `eami-api/internal/api/bootstrap_test.go`'s already-proven per-test throwaway-database pattern (`CREATE DATABASE`/apply real migrations/`DROP DATABASE`) for `eami-gateway`'s real-Postgres tests closes **both** this entry's orphaned-row accumulation problem **and** B-140's hash-chain race in one motion — full per-test isolation means nothing is left behind to orphan, and no cross-package interference is possible by construction. Option (b) ("accept and document") is no longer the pragmatic cheaper choice now that the same root cause has demonstrated it can also produce visible, confusing CI failures, not just silent row accumulation. **Fix sketch's open decision is resolved: pursue isolated/ephemeral test databases (option a).** Not yet built — this becomes a real, contained follow-up brief whenever picked up (see B-140's acceptance criteria for the concrete shape), not urgent (production is provably unaffected by either this entry's or B-140's finding) but no longer purely theoretical. See B-140's full investigation record.

### B-123 — `eami-gateway/internal/workflow`'s `allowAllRulesRealPolicyID()` is now a byte-identical duplicate of `allowAllRules()`, with a stale doc comment
**Objective:** found by the mandatory code-review pass on B-115/B-117 — `allowAllRulesRealPolicyID()` (added by B-093 as a local workaround before B-115's fix existed) is now character-for-character identical to the now-fixed `allowAllRules()` (both use `uuid.NewString()` for the rule ID). Its doc comment still asserts `allowAllRules()`'s literal `"allow-all"` string isn't a valid UUID — no longer true since B-115. Correctly left untouched by B-115 (the task brief's MUST NOT MODIFY explicitly named "any other test helper"), but now genuinely redundant.
**Severity:** Low — pure cleanup, zero functional impact, lowest priority of this session's 3 new findings per explicit user direction.
**Fix sketch:** delete `allowAllRulesRealPolicyID()` and repoint its 2 callers (`TestExecutor_Run_AuditLogRecordsWorkflowRunIDAndStepIndex` and one other, per B-093's original entry) at `allowAllRules()` directly.
**Dependencies:** B-115 (the fix that made this redundant).

### B-128 — No `org_id` scoping exists anywhere in the policy evaluation pipeline: every tenant shares one global, unfiltered policy list — **DONE, 2026-09-01**
**Objective:** found while live-verifying B-124/125 against the real deployed stack — a seeded escalate-all policy for a fresh test org (`b124-liveverify`, priority 1, then priority -100) repeatedly failed to match a real dispatch, which led to inspecting exactly what rule set the running gateway was actually evaluating against. Confirmed directly from source, not inferred:
- `eami-gateway/internal/policyloader/loader.go`'s `queryRules()` (lines 114-129): `SELECT p.id, p.name, p.priority, p.action, p.alert, pc.* FROM policies p LEFT JOIN policy_conditions pc ON pc.policy_id = p.id WHERE p.status = 'active'` — no `org_id` predicate anywhere in the query, and `org_id` isn't even in the SELECT list.
- `eami-policy/types.go`: `Rule`, `Conditions`, and `ActionContext` (the full input to `Evaluator.Evaluate`) — none of the three has an `OrgID` field. There is no data available downstream to filter or match on even if the loader wanted to.
- `Loader.store()` builds exactly **one** `policy.Evaluator` from that single global, unfiltered rule list, held behind one `atomic.Pointer[evaluatorHolder]`. `Loader.Evaluator()` (called from the dispatch path) hands back that same single process-wide instance for every org's every dispatch.
- Confirmed live in the shared dev database: a direct `psql` run of the loader's own query returned 3 active policies from at least 2 different orgs — mine (`b124-liveverify`'s `b124-escalate-all`) plus a different org's `manual verify` and `Escalate Claude connector calls` — all competing in the same global priority-ordered list.
**Severity:** URGENT/Critical — this is a multi-tenant isolation failure in the policy engine itself, not test-fixture noise: in production, today, any org's dispatch can match (be allowed/denied/escalated by), or be silently shadowed by priority ordering against, any *other* org's policy. This is the core enforcement surface of the product.
**Fix sketch:** needs a real design pass, not a quick patch — likely `org_id` added to `policies`-adjacent query, `policy.Rule`/`ActionContext`, and either (a) the loader building a per-org `Evaluator` (keyed map instead of one global instance) or (b) `Evaluate` taking an org-scoped rule subset filtered before evaluation. Whoever picks this up should also re-examine whether any other cross-org policy interaction (alerting, reload notification scope) has the same gap, since `queryRules()` is the single source both the live evaluator and (per `internal/policyloader`'s doc comment) any future per-org tooling would read from.
**Dependencies:** none — pre-existing, not introduced by B-124/125 or any other work this session. **Blocks:** nothing structurally (B-124/125's own code is independently proven via 14+ real-Postgres Go integration tests), but discovery of this bug is why B-124/125's own *live* verification step is currently paused — see B-124/125 entries above for status.
**Status:** logged only, not fixed. Reported to the user with full evidence before minting, per this session's standing "confirm B-ID before minting" practice; user explicitly directed this be logged immediately as URGENT/Critical and committed/pushed on its own, ahead of any other work.

**Resolution (2026-09-01): DONE.** A separate investigation pass (no code changes) first re-confirmed all of the above still held against current code post-B-129, then empirically re-proved the leak live against the real dev DB (a throwaway probe, run once and deleted, showed a dispatch structurally belonging to org `b121-liveverify` matched and was decided by Dev Org's own "Escalate Claude connector calls" policy). Investigation recommended, and the user approved, the minimal additive design over a per-org-evaluator split: real deployment-scale signals (`ARCHITECTURE.md` §8's dedicated-Postgres-per-customer topology) confirmed per-org evaluators would be unneeded complexity for this product's actual scale.

**What shipped (exactly the confirmed 4-point design, no interface changes):**
1. `eami-gateway/internal/policyloader/loader.go`'s `queryRules()` now selects `p.org_id` and scans it onto the constructed `policy.Rule.OrgID`.
2. `eami-policy/types.go`: `OrgID string` added to both `Rule` and `ActionContext` (empty is a wildcard only for hand-built test literals — a DB-loaded rule's `OrgID` is never empty, `policies.org_id` is `NOT NULL`).
3. `eami-policy/structural.go`'s `matchesRule()` gained one new, unconditional, first check: `if r.OrgID != "" && r.OrgID != ac.OrgID { return false }`.
4. `eami-gateway/internal/mcp/handler.go`'s `ToPolicyContext()` now sets `OrgID: a.OrgID` — confirmed server-resolved from `sess.Agent.OrgID`, never client input, at every real construction site.

No schema/migration work (`policies.org_id` has existed since the original schema). `policy.EvaluatorSource`'s interface (B-129) and `ReorderPolicies`/priority-ordering logic (B-086/090) both confirmed completely untouched, and both re-verified live afterward with zero interaction.

**One real regression found and fixed by the mandatory review passes, before shipping:** `eami-gateway/internal/workflow/executor.go`'s `runStep` builds an independent, informational `policy.ActionContext` purely to populate `StepResult.ProjectedDecision` (the audit-facing preview field) — this literal never set `OrgID`, so once every real rule gained a non-empty `OrgID`, the preview could never match any real policy again, silently showing `"allow"` on every workflow step regardless of the org's actual policy. Confirmed **not** a security hole (the real, enforced dispatch two lines below correctly inherits `OrgID` from `template` and was never affected) — a silent observability/audit-data regression only, but introduced by this very fix and caught by both review passes independently. Fixed with one line (`OrgID: template.OrgID`); a new regression test (`TestExecutor_Run_ProjectedDecision_OrgScopedRule_StillMatches`, `eami-gateway/internal/workflow/executor_test.go`) uses org-scoped (non-wildcard) rules specifically so this class of regression can't hide behind the existing `escalateRiskyRules()` helper's `OrgID`-less wildcard rules again — verified to fail with the exact predicted symptom (`ProjectedDecision = "allow"`, want `"escalate"`) when the one-line fix is reverted, then verified to pass with it restored.

**A second, more severe finding surfaced by the security review — pre-existing, NOT introduced by this fix, disclosed and NOT fixed here: see B-141.** The security review found that `eami-gateway/internal/registry.go`'s `LookupByName` (used by MCP session creation, workflow run creation, and the episode bearer-JWT auth path) resolves an agent by name alone, with no `org_id` predicate — and `gateway_agents` only enforces `UNIQUE(org_id, name)`, not global name uniqueness. Two different orgs can legitimately create same-named agents (e.g. both named "prod-agent"), and `LookupByName`'s unordered, unscoped query means the wrong org's row can be resolved, which would corrupt `ac.OrgID` **at its source** — silently defeating this very fix's org check, since the check is only as trustworthy as the identity resolution that fills `ac.OrgID` in the first place. This is a pre-existing, deeper bug B-042 (2026-08-04) partially addressed for the token-revoke endpoint only (`LookupByNameAndOrg`) but explicitly left this same unscoped path in place for `internal/mcp`/`internal/episode`/`internal/workflow`, reasoning that JWT signature verification alone made it safe — the security review found that reasoning has a real hole (the JWT's `sub` carries only a name string, never an org ID, and the DB constraint permits the exact cross-org name collision the old reasoning assumed away). Confirmed real (not the B-128 class of bug — one layer earlier, in identity resolution, not policy evaluation) and logged as its own item, **not fixed in this brief** (large design decision — a JWT org-binding claim, or moving every `LookupByName` caller to org-scoped resolution the way B-042 did for revoke — correctly out of this fix's scope).

**Test coverage — the adversarial two-org proof required by explicit user direction, matching B-042's own standard:**
- **AC1 (centerpiece):** `TestDispatch_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsConflictingPolicies` (`eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/dispatcher_org_scoping_test.go`, new) — two real orgs, genuinely conflicting same-priority policies on the identical tool name (Org A denies, Org B allows), both loaded into the one real, shared `*policyloader.Loader`/`*Dispatcher` a production process actually runs. Org A's dispatch is denied by its own policy; Org B's identical-tool dispatch is allowed by its own policy, completely unaffected by Org A's rule.
- **AC2 (negative control):** `TestLoad_OrgScoping_CrossOrgDispatchNoLongerMatches` (`eami-gateway/internal/policyloader/loader_pg_test.go`, new) — reproduces the investigation's exact repro (a dispatch for one org matching another org's real `tool_names: [claude]` escalate policy) and confirms it no longer matches post-fix, with a same-org positive control in the same test ruling out an overly-broad filter.
- **AC3 (no regression):** full `go test ./...` for both `eami-gateway` and `eami-policy` against real Postgres, 100% green, including the pre-existing `TestLoad_ToolServerIDs_RealDBRoundTrip` (updated to set `OrgID` on its `ActionContext` literals now that it needs to, per the code review's own catch — without the update it would have started silently passing for the wrong reason instead of testing `ToolServerIDs` matching).
- **AC4 (B-129 hot-reload, unaffected):** the existing `dispatcher_policy_reload_test.go` (B-129's own adversarial before/after pair) already inserts its policy with a real `org_id` matching its dispatch's `OrgID` and re-passed unmodified — no interface change to `EvaluatorSource` means B-129's fresh-fetch-per-dispatch mechanism needed no changes at all.
- **AC5 (B-086/090 priority, unaffected):** `TestEvaluate_OrgScoping_PriorityOrderingIndependentPerOrg` (`eami-policy/policy_test.go`, new, unit-level) proves two orgs' rules interleaved in one sorted evaluator resolve using only their own priority ordering. Live re-run of `eami-api`'s full reorder/policy test suite (`TestReorderPolicies_*`, `TestListPolicies_*`, etc.) against real Postgres — all pass unmodified, confirming zero interaction (expected: `ReorderPolicies` is a completely separate CRUD-layer code path, never touched).

**Live-verified:** all of the above against the real dev Postgres; additionally the actual `eami-gateway` Docker image was rebuilt and the container restarted twice (once after the core fix, once after the review-pass fixes), confirmed clean startup both times (`"policyloader: loaded policies from DB","count":2"`, no query errors against the real production schema). All test fixtures (orgs/policies/agents) confirmed cleaned up via `t.Cleanup`, zero leftover rows.

**Mandatory reviewer + security passes, both ran, both found real issues (the executor.go regression above, independently, plus the security review's B-141 finding), all disclosed, the in-scope one fixed before shipping.** Full findings in the session transcript.

**Dependencies:** none. **Unblocks:** B-124/125 (already DONE, live-verified 2026-08-28 against an environment that still had this gap — that verification's own disclosure is now moot, the gap is closed).

### B-141 — `registry.LookupByName`'s cross-org agent-name collision can corrupt identity resolution at its source, defeating B-128's org check upstream of policy evaluation — **DONE, 2026-09-01**
**Objective:** `eami-gateway/internal/registry/registry.go`'s `queryByName` (backing `LookupByName`, used by MCP SSE session creation in `internal/mcp/handler.go`, workflow run creation in `internal/workflow/http.go`, and the episode bearer-JWT auth path in `internal/episode/http.go`) resolves an agent by `name` alone: `SELECT id, org_id, name, scope, risk_tier, status FROM gateway_agents WHERE name = $1 LIMIT 1` — no `org_id` predicate, no deterministic `ORDER BY`. `gateway_agents` only enforces `UNIQUE(org_id, name)` (confirmed: `eami-api`'s `CreateAgent` only rejects a duplicate name within the same org, not globally), so two different orgs can legitimately create identically-named agents (realistic names: "prod-agent", "claude", "support-bot"). Whichever org's row Postgres's unordered scan happens to return "wins" the collision for every caller resolving that name — including the caller whose real, validly-signed JWT was issued for the *other* org's same-named agent.
**Why this matters more than a routine bug:** B-128's fix (`eami-policy`'s new org-scoped `matchesRule` check) is only as trustworthy as `ac.OrgID`'s provenance. `ac.OrgID` traces back through `mcp.ActionContext`/`workflow` templates to exactly this `LookupByName` resolution. If the wrong org's row is resolved here, B-128's org check still runs correctly — against the *wrong* org, silently. Concretely: policy decisions land under the wrong org, `audit_log`/`episodes` rows get written under the wrong `org_id`/`agent_id`, escalation approval notifications (containing real tool-call parameters) route to the wrong org's approvers, and — most severe — the episode bearer-JWT auth path uses this same resolution to scope which org's `GET /v1/gateway/episodes` content a request can **read**, meaning a self-serve customer who deliberately picks a colliding agent name has a plausible path to reading another org's raw episode content (tool-call arguments and results). The 30s TTL, name-keyed registry cache makes this durable rather than merely racy: repeatedly hitting the lookup with one's own valid JWT can pin the shared cache entry to one's own org's row.
**Pre-existing, not introduced by this session's B-128 fix** — found only because B-128's security review specifically traced `ac.OrgID`'s provenance rather than trusting the "server-resolved, never client input" claim on faith. B-042 (2026-08-04) fixed this exact bug class for the token-revoke endpoint (`LookupByNameAndOrg`, org-scoped) but explicitly left `internal/mcp`/`internal/episode` on the unscoped path, reasoning that JWT signature verification alone made a name-only lookup safe. That reasoning has a real hole: a JWT's `sub` claim carries only the name string, never an org ID or agent UUID — signature verification proves the gateway minted that name claim for *some* org at issuance, not that a later name-only lookup resolves back to that same org's row. `internal/workflow/http.go`'s `HandleRun` (added after B-042) inherited the same unscoped call and was never covered by B-042's reasoning at all.
**Fix sketch, not yet decided — a real design decision, not a one-line patch:** either (a) the JWT gains a tamper-evident `org_id` claim set at issuance, verified against the resolved agent's actual `org_id` at every lookup (not merely used to skip the lookup), or (b) every `LookupByName` caller moves to org-scoped resolution the way B-042 did for revoke — which requires each caller to already know the org before resolving the agent (may not hold for every call site; needs investigation per site).
**Severity:** Critical — same tier as B-128 itself, arguably more severe since it can defeat B-128's fix without touching `eami-policy` at all, and has a plausible cross-tenant **read** path (episode content), not just a decision-routing confusion.
**Dependencies:** none. **Status:** logged only, not fixed — disclosed to the user per this session's standing practice; needs a founder decision on fix direction (JWT claim vs. per-call-site org-scoping) before a build brief is scoped.

**Resolution (2026-09-01): DONE.** A dedicated investigation pass first traced all 4 real call sites and confirmed, precisely, that none of them had an org_id available prior to the lookup (the lookup itself is what discovers it) — meaning per-call-site scoping alone was structurally impossible without JWT org-binding first; the two "fix directions" from the original entry were not independent alternatives, JWT-binding is the prerequisite that makes per-call-site scoping achievable at all. The investigation also traced B-042's original "safe by construction" reasoning precisely: it held for the two call sites that predate B-042 (`mcp/handler.go`, `episode/http.go`, both explicitly named in B-042's own doc comment) only in the sense that B-042 declined to fix them, not that the reasoning was actually sound — and two NEW unscoped call sites (`workflow/http.go`, B-059; `workflow/ratelimit.go`, B-070) were added **after** B-042 already existed as a documented precedent in the same file, neither reusing it. The cross-tenant episode-content read was concretely traced (not just asserted): `episode/http.go`'s bearer-JWT `authenticateCaller` used the resolved (possibly wrong-org) record's `OrgID` as the *only* value scoping which org's `GET /v1/gateway/episodes` content a request could read.

**What shipped (user-approved plan, B5 leading, B6 dependent on it):**
1. `eami-gateway/internal/identity/tokens.go`: `Claims` (JWT custom claims) and `IssueRequest` both gained `OrgID string` — `IssueRequest.OrgID` has `json:"-"` (never settable via a client request body, confirmed empirically: a standalone decode test with both `"org_id"` and `"OrgID"` keys in the JSON body left the field empty both times). `Manager.Issue` signs `OrgID: req.OrgID` into the token, same additive pattern as B-116's existing Scope/Task/Model/Owner/RiskTier rebuild.
2. `eami-gateway/internal/identity/issue_http.go`: `HandleIssue` sets `req.OrgID = key.OrgID` (the validated API key's own org, from `ValidateAndResolveAgent`'s already org-scoped query) immediately before `Manager.Issue`, alongside the existing B-116 block.
3. All 4 confirmed call sites switched from `LookupByName` to the pre-existing, already-correct, untouched `LookupByNameAndOrg` (B-042): `mcp/handler.go` ServeSSE, `workflow/http.go` HandleRun, `workflow/ratelimit.go` RateLimitRunMiddleware, `episode/http.go` authenticateCaller.
4. **Hard cutover, as recommended and confirmed before building:** a token with an empty `OrgID` claim (pre-fix-shaped) is rejected with 401 at 3 of the 4 sites (ServeSSE, HandleRun, episode read). `RateLimitRunMiddleware` deliberately does NOT hard-reject — confirmed with the user as a pre-existing, safe design property (best-effort, already falls through to `next()` on any resolution failure; `HandleRun`'s own independent `LookupByNameAndOrg` call, wired directly as `next` with nothing in between per `main.go`'s real routing, is the actual, unconditional security boundary) — both mandatory review passes independently traced and confirmed this asymmetry is genuinely safe, not just documented as such.
5. `registry.LookupByNameAndOrg` itself, `revoke_http.go`'s already-correct usage, B-128's policy-scoping fix, B-098's cross-agent-scoping check, and B-116's claims-rebuild pattern all confirmed completely untouched, per the brief's explicit MUST NOT MODIFY list — B-098's `TestIssueHandler_SameAgentNameDifferentOrgs_ResolvesOwnOrgOnly` and B-042's `TestRevokeHandler_CrossOrgAgentName_Returns403` both re-run live, unaffected.

**Test coverage — the adversarial three-surface proof required by explicit user direction, matching B-128's own two-org standard, all against real Postgres:**
- **AC1 (centerpiece, all 3 surfaces):** `TestServeSSE_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsSameAgentName_ResolvesCorrectAgentEachTime` (`cmd/gateway/mcp_org_scoping_test.go`, new) captures the real `mcp.ActionContext` a real tool_call dispatch receives and confirms OrgID/AgentUUID match the correct org, never the other's identically-named agent. `TestHandleRun_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsSameAgentName_ResolvesCorrectAgentEachTime` (`internal/workflow/http_org_scoping_test.go`, new — HandleRun's first-ever direct test coverage) confirms via the real `workflow_runs.org_id`/`agent_id` columns. `TestListEpisodes_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsSameAgentName_NeverLeaksAcrossOrgs` (`internal/episode/http_org_scoping_pg_test.go`, new) confirms Org A's own token sees its own episode.
- **AC2 (negative control), verified real, not just asserted:** the same episode test's second half reproduces the exact traced leak scenario (Org B's token, presenting the identical agent name, must not see Org A's episode) — and this was empirically confirmed to actually catch the bug: the fix was manually reverted (episode's `AgentResolver` swapped back to the old unscoped `LookupByName`), the test re-run, and it failed with the exact predicted leak (`"Org B's token received Org A's episode (id=..., task=\"b141-secret-org-a-task\")"`), then the fix was restored and the test passed again.
- **AC3 (no regression):** full `go test ./...` for `eami-gateway`, 100% green, zero regressions.
- **AC4 (hard cutover):** dedicated pre-cutover-token tests at all 4 sites — 401 at ServeSSE/HandleRun/episode-read, confirmed pass-through-then-real-403-from-HandleRun at RateLimitRunMiddleware.
- **AC5 (B-128/B-098/B-116 unaffected):** targeted re-run by name of `TestDispatch_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsConflictingPolicies`/`TestLoad_OrgScoping_CrossOrgDispatchNoLongerMatches` (B-128), `TestIssueHandler_SameAgentNameDifferentOrgs_ResolvesOwnOrgOnly` and the full `TestIssueHandler_*` suite (B-098/B-107/B-118), `TestRevokeHandler_CrossOrgAgentName_Returns403` (B-042) — all pass unmodified.
- `internal/workflow/ratelimit_test.go`'s pre-existing `TestRateLimitRunMiddleware_SameName_DifferentOrgs_IndependentLimits` test was strengthened, not just adapted: its old context-smuggling test-only device (`ctxOrgResolver`/`ctxOrgKey`, needed because production code had no real mechanism to distinguish two same-named agents) is now genuinely obsolete and was deleted, replaced with two real tokens carrying distinct real `OrgID` claims — a strictly stronger version of the same test, made possible by this fix.

**Mandatory reviewer + security passes, both ran, both clean — no correctness or security defects found in the fix.** Both independently converged on the same single finding, informational only: `registry.go`'s `LookupByNameAndOrg` doc comment (lines 104-108) still asserts the original, now-disproven "safe by construction" reasoning about `internal/mcp`/`internal/episode`'s old `LookupByName` usage — factually stale now that both are migrated, and a landmine for a future engineer who might read it as precedent for a new unscoped call site. `registry.go` is outside this brief's MAY MODIFY scope, so left as-is and logged fresh as **B-142** rather than fixed inline.

**Live-verified:** all of the above against the real dev Postgres; the actual `eami-gateway` Docker image rebuilt and container restarted, confirmed clean startup against the real production schema; all test fixtures confirmed cleaned up, zero leftover rows.

**Dependencies:** none. **New discovered work:** B-142 (Low, stale doc comment + now-dead `LookupByName` code, logged not fixed).

### B-129 — Policy hot-reload is completely non-functional on the actual dispatch path: only a full gateway restart makes any policy change take effect, for any org — **DONE, 2026-08-28**
**Objective:** found while root-causing why B-128's investigation still couldn't get a live escalate policy to match even after two confirmed successful `pg_notify` reloads. Ruled out the mundane explanation first, per explicit user direction, before considering anything more complex:
- **Stale process/cache/build — ruled out.** The `eami-gateway` container and image were both freshly built ~10 minutes before either dispatch attempt (image `Created: 2026-08-28T04:27:30Z`, container `StartedAt: 04:27:34Z`). Gateway logs show `"policyloader: LISTEN policy_reload active"` at startup, then two separate, successful reloads (`04:28:07` and `04:30:15`, both logging `"loaded policies from DB","count":3`), each landing *before* its respective dispatch attempt (`04:28:32`, `04:30:32`). `pLoader`'s own internal atomic-pointer state was genuinely, correctly updated both times — this is not a build or caching lag.
- **Real cause — confirmed by an exhaustive repo-wide grep for `.Evaluator()`: exactly 2 production call sites in the whole module, both in `main.go`, both at startup, neither ever called again:**
  - `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/main.go:212`: `dispatcher := NewDispatcher(toolRouter, aiProviderRouter, pLoader.Evaluator(), ...)`
  - `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/main.go:244`: `workflowExecutor := workflow.New(pool, dispatcher.Dispatch, pLoader.Evaluator())`
  - Both snapshot the `policy.Evaluator` interface value ONCE at construction. `Dispatcher.policyEval` (`dispatcher.go:151`) stores that frozen value; `Dispatch()` (`dispatcher.go:412`) calls `d.policyEval.Evaluate(reqCtx, pc)` against it forever after — never re-fetched.
  - `pLoader.Listen()` (`internal/policyloader/loader.go:83-111`) correctly updates `pLoader`'s own `atomic.Pointer[evaluatorHolder]` on every `pg_notify policy_reload`, exactly as designed — but nothing downstream of startup ever calls `pLoader.Evaluator()` again to observe that update.
  - **This is a confirmed, direct violation of the package's own documented contract.** `internal/policyloader/loader.go`'s own top-of-file doc comment (line 11) spells out the intended usage: `decision, _ := loader.Evaluator().Evaluate(ctx, ac.ToPolicyContext())` — called fresh, per dispatch, "in the dispatch closure." `main.go` does not follow it.
  - Startup logs a directly false claim as a result: `"policy engine ready (DB-backed, live reload enabled)","rules":N` — reload is enabled and functional for `pLoader`'s own internal state (and thus `pLoader.RuleCount()`), but not for the one thing that matters: what `Dispatch()` actually evaluates against.
**Severity:** Critical — per explicit user direction, more urgent than B-128 and far more urgent than B-124/125. Distinct from B-128: this affects every org's every policy change (create, edit, reorder via B-090, delete), not just cross-org interference — any policy administered through the real running product silently does nothing to live dispatch decisions until someone restarts the gateway process, with zero error, zero warning, and a startup log line actively asserting the opposite is true. Also fully explains why B-124/125's own live-verification attempts could not succeed regardless of policy priority.
**Investigation findings (answered before building, per explicit user direction, all confirmed rather than assumed):**
1. **Performance was not a legitimate reason for the snapshot-once pattern.** `Loader.Evaluator()` is a plain `atomic.Pointer.Load()` — all real cost (the query, sort, rule-slice build) already happens inside `Load()`/`store()`, not in `Evaluator()`. The one real constraint was test isolation: `dispatcher_test.go` injects a `&fakeEvaluator{}` directly at 2 call sites with no real Postgres needed — changing `Dispatcher` to hold a concrete `*policyloader.Loader` would have broken that.
2. **`workflowExecutor` has two independent instances of the bug, not one.** Its real *enforcement* flows through the same `dispatcher.Dispatch` closure (identical exposure, one fix covers both) — but it also holds its own separate frozen snapshot for `StepResult.ProjectedDecision` (a persisted, API-exposed, non-enforcing preview field, `workflow_run_steps.projected_decision`), captured at a second, independent `main.go` call site. Both needed fixing.
3. **Tonight's prior live-verification claims (B-086, B-090, B-098, B-116, B-118) are genuinely unaffected**, confirmed by reading each one's actual `BUILT.md` write-up rather than assuming: B-086/B-090 proved the `eami-api` reorder *endpoint*/DB-write correctness via direct `psql` reads, never touching `eami-gateway`'s live evaluator; B-098/B-116/B-118 are identity/token-issuance paths with zero policy evaluation involved. B-121's live verification is the one genuinely uncertain case (seeded a Deny policy, but the specific claim it proved — the `AuditDecision` field's correctness — didn't depend on that policy actually matching) — flagged as an open, undecided question, not silently resolved either way.
**What shipped:** `eami-policy/evaluator.go` gained a new `EvaluatorSource` interface (`Evaluator() Evaluator`) with a doc comment explaining the failure mode by name. `*policyloader.Loader` already satisfies it structurally (its existing `Evaluator()` method matches) — a `var _ policy.EvaluatorSource = (*Loader)(nil)` compile-time assertion makes that explicit. `Dispatcher` (`dispatcher.go`) and `workflow.Executor` (`executor.go`) both had their `policyEval policy.Evaluator` field renamed to `policyEvalSource policy.EvaluatorSource`, with both call sites now doing `.policyEvalSource.Evaluator().Evaluate(...)` fresh, not cached. `main.go` now passes `pLoader` itself (not `pLoader.Evaluator()`) into both `NewDispatcher(...)` and `workflow.New(...)`. Test files (`dispatcher_test.go`, `internal/workflow/testenv_test.go`) gained a small `staticEvaluatorSource` adapter so existing fake-evaluator-based tests keep working unchanged.
**Test coverage — the adversarial before/after proof required by explicit user direction:** `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/dispatcher_policy_reload_test.go` (real Postgres) has two tests sharing identical setup against the same real `Dispatcher`/`policyloader.Loader`, differing in exactly one line — the variable under test: `TestDispatch_PolicyReload_TakesEffectWithoutRestart` (passes `pLoader` itself) proves a Deny policy inserted and reloaded *after* construction, with no restart, denies the very next dispatch on the same `Dispatcher` instance. `TestDispatch_PolicyReload_FrozenSnapshotWiring_ReproducesTheBug` (passes `staticEvaluatorSource{ev: pLoader.Evaluator()}` — a byte-for-byte reproduction of the old `main.go` pattern) proves the identical scenario still silently allows under the old wiring shape. Both pass today, isolating the fix to the one real causal variable rather than merely asserting the bug used to exist. `eami-gateway/internal/policyloader/loader_bench_test.go` empirically confirmed the performance question rather than assuming it: the fresh `.Evaluator()` call costs ~1.5ns vs. ~0.4ns for a cached value (both 0 allocations) — about 0.04% of a single `Evaluate()` call's own ~2700ns, utterly negligible against the full dispatch path's real DB/HTTP round trips.
**Mandatory reviewer + security passes, both ran (each needed relaunching once after hitting a session/usage limit, same recovery pattern as earlier in this session), both found zero correctness or security defects in the fix itself.** Security review's one substantive finding was that this fix changes the *blast radius* of the already-logged, out-of-scope **B-128** (no `org_id` scoping in policy evaluation): an over-broad policy from any org now reaches live enforcement for every org on the very next dispatch instead of only after the next restart — correctly flagged as B-128's concern, not a new defect in this diff, and not fixed here (B-128 remains open, separately). Code review found two non-blocking style notes only (a pre-existing, unrelated `gofmt` misalignment left untouched, and the compile-time assertion above, which was added). Both passes independently confirmed: no other call site in the repo (including `eami-api`, `eami-collector`) held a frozen `policy.Evaluator`; the rename was applied with zero stale references (repo-wide grep); the new adversarial test's `t.Cleanup(pool.Close)` ordering follows CLAUDE.md's mandatory pattern with no leaked rows (cascade-deleted via `policies.org_id REFERENCES orgs(id) ON DELETE CASCADE`); `.Evaluator()` is called exactly once per `Dispatch()` call (no new intra-dispatch TOCTOU window) and `Loader.Evaluator()` is nil-safe before any `Load()` (falls back to default-Allow, identical fail-open behavior to the old code's startup window, self-healing on first successful reload where the old code never did).
**Live-verified against the real rebuilt/restarted gateway container, reproducing and resolving the exact scenario that blocked B-124/125's own live verification:** a fresh org/admin/agent/API-key seeded, a real agent JWT issued, a real SSE session opened, a first `tool_call` dispatched (no policy yet — Allow-branch, proxy failure since no downstream configured in this dev topology, same established pattern as B-121's own live verification). A real Deny policy (`priority 1`, empty conditions) then inserted directly into Postgres and `pg_notify('policy_reload','')` fired — **no gateway restart, no rebuild**. A second dispatch on the exact same running process was then genuinely denied: gateway logs show `"policy denied [...]: matched rule \"b129-deny-all\" (priority 1)"`, confirmed via direct `psql` against the real `audit_log` table. All fixtures cleaned up via `DELETE FROM orgs` cascade, confirmed 0 remaining.
**Dependencies:** none — pre-existing, not introduced by B-124/125, B-128, or any other work this session. Found via the same live-verification attempt as B-128 but is a structurally distinct bug (auth/scoping gap vs. a stale-snapshot wiring bug). **Unblocks:** B-124/125's own live verification, paused since B-128/B-129 were found — resumes next.

### B-130 — EPIC: Native Governed AI Desktop Client ("the Claude Desktop replacement") — **logged, investigation not started**
**Objective:** a first-party desktop application, installed on end-user machines, that becomes THE interaction surface for AI at a customer org — replacing the need for employees to use ungoverned consumer AI tools at all, rather than just detecting when they do (the endpoint agent's current job). Provider-agnostic: connects to Claude, Gemini, other cloud providers, OR a customer's own self-hosted/in-house-deployed LLM, all governed identically. Two distinct interaction modes, explicitly different sizes, never combined into one estimate: (1) a chat interface (like Claude Desktop) and (2) a coding-agent interface (like Claude Code) — the second is categorically larger and must be scoped as its own later phase.
**Critical architectural principle, must anchor every future brief under this epic:** the desktop client is a THIN CLIENT to the existing gateway, never an independent decision-maker. Every message, every AI call, every allow/deny/escalate/redact decision is made server-side by the already-built, already-adversarially-tested policy engine, approval flow, audit trail, and cost tracking (B-039 onward, B-057, B-097-112, B-121-125). The client's job is to be a fast, polished window onto governance that already exists — not a second trust boundary to independently secure. This reuses the AI-provider adapter architecture (B-057/B-103, see also this file's "Pluggable backend types" convention in `CLAUDE.md`) for real, since the gateway (not the client) decides which backend to route to per policy. Confirms and builds on the standing fact already in `CONTEXT.md`: the Bearer AI-token JWT dual-auth path was built with no live consumer specifically anticipating this.
**Confirmed additional capabilities envisioned (not yet scoped into briefs):** admin can push policies to the client fleet; real-time usage monitoring; publish "skills" company-wide through it — effectively a management/distribution channel for governed AI capability, not just a chat window.
**Real new complexity, flagged explicitly, not hidden:** this is a THIRD installed client (alongside the existing endpoint agent and browser extension). Real cross-platform desktop packaging (likely Electron or Tauri), code signing, auto-update infrastructure, and enterprise deployment (SCCM/Intune/Jamf, matching the existing endpoint-agent installer precedent) is genuine new infrastructure, independent of the chat UI itself.
**Real risk, flagged explicitly:** per the Workflow Canvas epic's own expensive lesson (built, personally tested, found genuinely broken interactions no code review caught, fully reverted), a real-time streaming chat interface is arguably a HIGHER-risk interactive-UI surface than the canvas was. Any future brief under this epic must apply at least the same incremental, prove-it-in-stages discipline — even though the canvas was ultimately reverted after using that same discipline.
**Open technical question, not yet verified:** self-hosted/in-house LLM support is likely coverable by a single flexible "custom OpenAI-compatible endpoint" adapter (vLLM/Ollama/TGI/LocalAI commonly expose OpenAI-compatible APIs) rather than bespoke work per customer deployment — this compatibility claim needs real verification before being assumed, not taken for granted.
**Conversation-context architecture note:** LLMs are stateless — no model holds memory between calls. Any "the AI remembers our conversation" experience is entirely constructed by re-sending the growing transcript with every new message. Given the thin-client-to-gateway principle above, the GATEWAY (not the desktop client, not the AI provider) should own and re-inject conversation history on each turn — this ensures every message in a conversation's full context has already passed through policy/audit once, not just the latest message in isolation.
