### B-155 — EPIC: Conditional workflow branching (structural + LLM-based validation) — **logged, investigation not started**
**Objective:** allow a workflow step to branch to different next steps based on a condition, evaluated either **(a)** against a specific field already extractable via B-063/064's existing jq-based extraction mechanism, or **(b)** by delegating judgment to a real LLM dispatch through the existing `ai_provider` pipeline (asking it to classify/judge the prior step's real output) — the LLM's own constrained answer becomes the typed value the engine branches on.
**Critical re-examination needed, not a settled question to inherit unquestioned:** the workflow engine's original finding (B-058 onward, reaffirmed as recently as B-131's own investigation) — that real branching is architecturally blocked because there are no typed connector output schemas to validate against — **predates B-063/064's extraction mechanism**. Whoever picks this up must re-investigate whether narrow, single-field structural conditions (mode (a) above) are now more tractable than that original finding assumed, rather than treating the old conclusion as still fully current just because it's been repeated in several intervening entries (B-064/065/066/067/068/131 all cite it, but none of them re-tested it against the extraction mechanism that shipped after the original finding).
**Real architectural insight, not a bolt-on:** LLM-based condition evaluation (mode (b)) fits the one-spine principle directly (`CLAUDE.md` Conventions) — it's just another governed Action, a real `ai_provider` dispatch through the existing pipeline, not new architecture, not a new identity/audit/UI surface. **Likely the more tractable of the two modes and may deserve building first** — it sidesteps the typed-schema problem entirely by asking the LLM to produce the typed branch value directly, rather than needing to structurally validate an arbitrary connector's raw output.
**Honest scope, stated plainly:** this changes the workflow execution engine's fundamental topology from strictly linear to a real branching graph — a genuine schema/execution-engine redesign, not a UI feature. `workflow_steps`' flat `UNIQUE(workflow_id, step_order)` schema (migration 000006) cannot represent this today; this is not a small brief.
**Real open questions for investigation, not yet answered:**
- Does a branch decision need its own tamper-evident audit record, per B-124/125's resolution-audit-write precedent — a branch taken is itself a governance-relevant decision, not just an execution detail?
- Does an LLM-based condition dispatch (mode (b)) need the same TOCTOU protections as any other governed action, per B-057 — since it's a real dispatch through the same pipeline, the existing precedent likely says yes, but this needs confirming against the actual mechanism, not assumed by analogy alone.
- How does this interact with `sequential-workflow-designer`'s own `SwitchStep` component — confirmed UI-side-only, informational, and not touching the backend gap per B-131's own investigation — once the backend can actually support real branching? Does `SwitchStep` become the real UI for this, or does the eventual backend model diverge from what `SwitchStep`'s shape assumes?
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is a scoping placeholder, not an actionable brief. First step for whoever picks this up is the re-examination above (structural mode) plus real investigation into the LLM-based mode's dispatch/audit/TOCTOU requirements, before any brief is written.
**Dependencies:** B-063/B-064 (extraction mechanism, the re-examination's real starting point), B-057 (TOCTOU precedent, to confirm applicability), B-124/B-125 (audit-write precedent, to confirm applicability), B-131 (`SwitchStep`'s UI-only status, to re-confirm once backend support might exist).
**Severity/Priority:** EPIC, comparable in scope to B-130/B-147 — its own dedicated investigation needed, not squeezed into an existing brief.
**Status:** discussed 2026-09-02, zero investigation or code started. (Note: this item was discussed in conversation earlier the same session but never actually sent as a task brief or logged — corrected here rather than left as an unresolved discrepancy from an earlier exchange this session.)

### B-156 — INVESTIGATION: pattern-based sensitive-data redaction on the `ai_provider` dispatch path — **investigation complete, no build brief started**
**Objective:** investigate adding pattern-based sensitive-data redaction (emails, SSNs, credit card numbers, API-key-shaped strings, admin-defined custom regex) to the existing `ai_provider` dispatch pipeline (B-057) — flagged as the single most repeated "important, unbuilt" gap across this session, and the one place the product's core "your data never leaves your building" claim is currently only half-true (every real Claude dispatch today sends raw, unredacted prompt content externally). **Explicitly scoped to pattern-based detection only** — semantic/ML-based detection is a distinct, later tier genuinely requiring the still-parked local-LLM capability (sending content to an external provider to judge whether it's safe to send to an external provider is circular), kept deliberately separate from this investigation.
**PART A — where this fits in the pipeline, traced against real code, not assumed:**
- **The true single chokepoint is `aiprovider.Router.Dispatch`** (`eami-gateway/internal/aiprovider/router.go:98-125`), specifically immediately before its `adapter.Dispatch(...)` call at line 124 — confirmed there are **two independent call sites** into it, not one: `cmd/gateway/dispatcher.go:669` (the immediate-Allow branch) and `internal/approval/router.go:836` (the escalated-then-approved resume path). A redaction check placed only in `Dispatcher.Dispatch`'s Allow branch would silently miss the escalation-resume path entirely — a real gap avoided by placing the check at the shared `Router.Dispatch` level instead, matching the one-spine principle (a new step on an existing Action, structurally identical to the function's existing early-return rejections for nil-row/no-provider/unregistered-adapter/undecryptable-credentials).
- **B-102's `DispatchHook` mechanism does NOT fit and should not be extended, not a "yes with modification."** Traced precisely (`cmd/gateway/dispatcher.go:134`, `717-719`): every hook runs strictly *after* `outcome` (containing the already-executed `Result`/`Err`) is fully built, and returns nothing — structurally observational-only, unable to block or modify, and by the time any hook runs the external call has already happened. Forking `DispatchHook` into a blocking variant would violate B-102's own explicit anti-generalization guard (`dispatcher.go:143-147`: *"do not generalize this into a repo-wide event-bus abstraction without a second, independently-confirmed site"*). **Recommendation: a plain, small, named interception step inside `Router.Dispatch` itself — not a new hook abstraction, not a reuse of B-102's type.**
**PART B — what gets redacted, and how:**
- **Library choice, verified via live research, not assumed:** real Go options exist (`go-phileas`, `pii-scrubber`, `pii-extractor`), and Microsoft Presidio's own architecture was confirmed (its `PatternRecognizer` = a named regex + an optional checksum/Luhn validator + a confidence score — a proven *design*, not a reason to adopt Presidio's Python/spaCy runtime). **Recommendation: replicate Presidio's proven pattern-design natively in Go using the stdlib `regexp` package** (RE2), zero new runtime dependency — for this narrow, well-defined entity set, full visibility into the exact matching logic on a compliance-critical path is worth more than a third-party dependency, matching this codebase's own established low-footprint discipline (B-144's `age`/`rclone` choice, B-148's `JSON.stringify`-over-a-deep-equal-library choice). The third-party libraries are real, known alternatives to revisit if the entity set ever grows toward broad/international PII coverage.
- **Tokenize vs. mask — recommend MASK for v1, tokenization deliberately deferred, not left unresolved.** Tokenization (reversible) gives a more coherent LLM response but introduces a genuinely new secret-management surface (a token↔value map, itself effectively a temporary decryption key) that deserves its own dedicated design/security pass, not a bolt-on to a first structural brief. Masking (irreversible) needs no storage, no lifecycle, no key — proving the interception mechanism itself is sound before adding tokenization's real complexity, per this session's own "prove it in stages" lesson from the Workflow Canvas epic.
**PART C — governance and visibility:**
- **A redaction count belongs in `audit_log` as a real, visible fact — never the redacted values.** `audit.Entry`'s hash formula (`internal/audit/writer.go:150-157`) is explicit and narrow (`prevHash+id+orgID+agentName+toolName+action+decision+timestamp`), deliberately excluding `Parameters`/`PolicyID`/`ApprovalID`/`ApprovedBy`/`LatencyMS`/`TokenIn/Out`/`DataHandling`/`WorkflowRunID`/`StepIndex` by the code's own listed comment. A new `RedactedCount`-shaped field belongs in this same category — a real, stored, visible column, **not** part of the tamper-evidence hash — exactly matching `DataHandling`'s (B-078) own established precedent and reasoning (`writer.go:61-69`: a visibility-only field, not a governance decision, gains nothing from being hashed).
- **Per-connector, not global — B-078 is the exact template, confirmed by tracing its real implementation.** `gateway_tools.data_handling_designation` (`schema/migrations-v2/000008...up.sql:19-21`) is a per-connector `TEXT` column with a `CHECK` allowlist, validated `ai_provider`-type-only in `eami-api/internal/api/tools.go`'s `CreateTool`/`UpdateTool`. Custom regex rules should follow `action_paths`' own pattern instead (`schema.sql:229-233`: nullable `JSONB`, `NULL` = default/no override) — a new `gateway_tools.redaction_rules JSONB` column, reusing a pattern this codebase already has twice over, not a new design. Recommended on real use-case grounds (different sensitivity per provider/connector), not just precedent-matching.
**PART D — security of the mechanism itself (relevant once/if tokenization is picked up):**
- **Precedent to reuse when this is scoped: `eami-api/internal/toolcreds` (encrypt) + `eami-gateway/internal/toolrouter/creds.go` (decrypt-only)** — application-level AES-256-GCM, key from an env var (hex-encoded 32 bytes), Postgres never sees plaintext or the key (explicit in the package's own doc comment: deliberately not `pgcrypto`, since that would expose the key alongside a DB-level compromise). A token-map needs identical treatment, not a new design.
- **Real open question, narrowed but not fully answered:** lifecycle. The map only needs to survive one request/response round-trip (redact outbound → dispatch → scan the real response for tokens → restore) — this dispatch path is explicitly non-streaming today (`aiprovider/types.go:47`), which argues for in-memory-only, never persisted, closing the "does this need its own encrypted table" question before it's asked — but needs confirming against real response shapes when actually scoped, not assumed from this reasoning alone.
**PART E — scope and sequencing:**
- **Honest estimate: a real multi-brief epic, not one contained brief.** Minimum real pieces: (1) the interception mechanism inside `Router.Dispatch` + built-in pattern set (email/SSN/CC/API-key) + mask-only + the new audit count field — proves the pipeline-integration risk first; (2) per-connector configurability (admin UI + `redaction_rules` JSONB, mirroring B-078's own `eami-ui` work) + custom regex; (3) tokenization, if and when chosen over masking, as its own dedicated brief with its own security review, given Part D's genuinely new secret-management surface. This touches the dispatch path's most security-critical surface directly — every brief here needs mandatory reviewer+security passes as a requirement, not an optional add-on.
**Acceptance criteria:** none — this entry is the investigation record only, per explicit instruction (investigation only, no code changes, no design decisions locked in). First step for whoever picks this up is Part E's Brief 1 (interception mechanism + built-in patterns + mask-only + audit count), with its own real ACs.
**Dependencies:** B-057 (`ai_provider` Adapter/dispatch pipeline, the pipeline this investigates), B-102 (dispatch-hook mechanism, confirmed NOT reused — a new small mechanism needed instead), B-078 (`DataHandling`/`data_handling_designation`, the direct precedent for both the audit-field and per-connector-config recommendations), B-121/B-124/B-125 (audit-write discipline, the precedent for the redaction-count-not-values recommendation), B-022/toolcreds (AES-256-GCM credential-encryption precedent, for tokenization if picked up later).
**Severity/Priority:** the single most repeated "important, unbuilt" gap flagged across this session, per explicit user framing — high strategic importance, but investigation-only tonight; no build brief minted.
**Status:** investigation complete, 2026-09-02, zero code changes. No B-ID for an actual build brief minted yet — Part E's Brief 1 would mint its own B-ID whenever picked up, same precedent as B-131's investigation record vs. its later B-145/B-148 build briefs.

### B-157 — EPIC: Modular licensing & entitlement system — **logged, investigation not started**
**Foundation:** the three-module product structure (Module 1: Discovery & Cost Visibility; Module 2: Gateway; Module 3: AI Infrastructure) needs a real entitlement system so modules can be independently enabled, disabled, and billed per customer.
**Core design challenge, must be resolved first, before any of the components below can be scoped for real:** this is an on-prem, potentially air-gapped appliance (ADR-020) — a typical SaaS "check a live billing API" approach is not available without compromising the core trust story. Real candidate: a cryptographically signed license file, verified locally, no live network dependency. **Open, real tradeoff to decide deliberately, not default:** fully offline-only (stronger trust story, harder to revoke/update remotely) vs. an optional periodic check-in (easier to manage, slightly weakens the "nothing phones home" story). This decision shapes everything else in this epic.
**Required components:**
1. **License data model** — which org, which modules, validity period, possibly usage/volume limits **as a separate axis from module-gating** (feature-gating and usage-metering are two different things, deliberately not conflated).
2. **Enforcement at the dispatch layer, per the one-spine principle** — an entitlement check is a gate an Action passes through, the same conceptual position as policy evaluation — never UI-only, since a direct API call could otherwise bypass a UI-only restriction. (Note: if B-156's redaction work or B-150's Guardrails work land first, whoever scopes this should check whether they've already established a clean "pre-dispatch gate" pattern worth reusing here, rather than inventing a third one independently.)
3. **RBAC × Licensing interaction** — two separate axes (who within an org, vs. what the org as a whole is entitled to) that must compose correctly. **Directly connects to and should resolve B-113** (`model_pricing` is a global table but writes are gated to any ordinary org "admin" role — a real, already-found cross-tenant gap that becomes more consequential once real billing is at stake). Consider a genuine platform-admin/billing-admin tier, distinct from the existing four RBAC roles.
4. **Visibility/UX** — hide unlicensed modules entirely vs. show with an upgrade prompt: a real go-to-market decision, not a technical default, needs the founder's explicit call when this is picked up.
5. **License administration UI** — reuses existing patterns (the Settings page), not a new invented pattern, per the UI-consistency mandate (item 11 below).
6. **Vendor-side license issuance** — how licenses actually get generated and delivered to a customer in the first place.
7. **Trial/expiry/grace-period behavior** — graceful degradation, warning windows, or hard-stop: needs a real, deliberate design, not a default.
8. **License changes are themselves real, audited events** — applied/upgraded/downgraded/expired, per this product's established audit discipline (B-121/B-124/B-125).
**Additional hard requirements, carried forward from prior discussion:**
9. **Cross-module lineage:** a model trained via Module 3 must carry an explicit, navigable link back to its originating training run/dataset once registered as a real `ai_provider` adapter in Module 2 — not automatic from the one-spine principle alone (the adapter pattern gives a place to dispatch through, not a lineage record), needs deliberate design.
10. **Identity-fragmentation priority escalation.** An earlier-session finding — that Discover's and Agents' identity models are "entirely disconnected" — is now materially more consequential, since Discovery (Module 1) and Gateway (Module 2) are separately-purchasable tiers: this gap sits at the literal seam between two paid modules. **Honest disclosure: this finding does not currently have its own logged B-ID anywhere in this backlog as of this session** (checked, not assumed) — referenced here from conversation, not from a formal existing entry; whoever picks this up should locate or re-establish the original finding's real scope before assuming this note alone is sufficient investigation. Recommend investigating and closing this **before or alongside** the entitlement system, not after.
11. **UI consistency mandate:** no future module invents its own UI pattern — every new page reuses the existing `DataTable` component (B-104), the cross-page deep-linking mechanism (B-092), and the existing detail-panel convention. Sophistication grows in capability; interaction patterns stay constant across every module.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is a scoping placeholder, not an actionable brief. First step for whoever picks this up is the core design challenge above (offline-only vs. periodic check-in), since it shapes every other component's real design.
**Dependencies:** B-113 (global `model_pricing` write-access gap, to resolve via component 3), B-104 (`DataTable`, reused per component 11), B-092 (deep-linking mechanism, reused per component 11), B-121/B-124/B-125 (audit-write discipline, for component 8), ADR-020 (on-prem/air-gapped deployment model, the core constraint behind the design challenge). The item-10 identity-fragmentation finding, once its real originating record is located or re-established.
**Severity/Priority:** foundational, business-critical EPIC, comparable in scope to B-130/B-147 — its own dedicated investigation needed, not built casually.
**Status:** discussed extensively 2026-09-02, real architectural direction identified, zero investigation or code started.

### B-146 — Auth session doesn't survive a hard page reload/direct URL navigation; login never redirects back to the originally-requested page — **DONE, 2026-09-02**
**Objective:** `eami-ui/src/stores/authStore.ts` holds `accessToken`/`refreshToken`/`isAuthenticated` in a plain in-memory Zustand store — no `persist` middleware, no localStorage/sessionStorage, no cookie (confirmed by direct grep across `authStore.ts`/`main.tsx`/`App.tsx`, and confirmed no `Set-Cookie` anywhere in `eami-api`'s login handler). Any hard browser navigation — a page refresh, a bookmarked link, a shared link, or simply typing/pasting a URL into the address bar — reboots the SPA from scratch and discards the session, even though the user's actual `refresh_token` may still be perfectly valid server-side. Compounding this, `LoginPage.tsx` unconditionally `navigate('/dashboard', { replace: true })`s after a successful login — it never captures or restores the page the user was actually trying to reach.
**How this was found:** discovered live while manually verifying B-145's new (deliberately unlinked, per that brief's own MAY-MODIFY scope) `/gateway/workflows/:id/canvas-preview` route — reaching it required a direct URL paste, which is a hard navigation, which surfaced this pre-existing gap for the first time in a way that was actually noticeable (a "redirect-to-login loop, even with a fresh login": paste URL → session lost → bounced to `/login` → log in → land on `/dashboard`, not the original URL → paste again → repeat). **Not a B-145 bug and not specific to that route** — the exact same thing happens on every other route in the app (`/gateway/policies`, `/audit`, etc.) if reached via a hard navigation instead of clicking through the Sidebar from an already-booted session; B-145 only made it visible because its new route has no Sidebar entry yet by design.
**Real-world impact, not just a dev-inconvenience:** any customer who refreshes their browser, opens a bookmarked deep link, or receives a shared link (e.g. a Slack approval-notification link, which `GATEWAY_UI_BASE_URL`/B-092's own deep-linking work already produce) currently loses their session and lands on the dashboard instead of the page the link pointed to — a real, live UX gap in already-shipped functionality, not a hypothetical.
**Fix sketch, two independent halves, not necessarily one brief:** (1) persist `accessToken`/`refreshToken` across a reload — options to weigh: Zustand's own `persist` middleware against `localStorage` (simplest, but a real XSS could read a JS-accessible token — worth a real security-tradeoff discussion given this product's own governance/audit positioning, not a default to reach for unreflectively), vs. moving to an HttpOnly cookie issued server-side by `eami-api`'s login/refresh handlers (more secure, more invasive — `openapi-fetch`'s `Authorization: Bearer` header injection in `client.ts` would need rework, plus CORS/`SameSite` implications for the Vite dev-server proxy vs. the production `eami-proxy` topology, both needing real verification, not assumption); (2) a real "redirect back to the originally-requested URL after login" flow (`AppShell`'s guard capturing `location.pathname` via `location.state`, `LoginPage.tsx` reading it back) — independent of and smaller than (1), worth shipping even if (1) is deferred, since it at least turns "session lost, dumped on dashboard" into "session lost, but you land back where you meant to go after logging back in."
**Resolution (2026-09-02):** persistence mechanism decided in favor of localStorage — the HttpOnly-cookie option needed `eami-api` changes that this fix's own scope (frontend-only, `authStore.ts`/`LoginPage.tsx`) explicitly forbade, so it was never actually available, not just less preferred. Shipped via Zustand's `persist` middleware, `localStorage`-backed. XSS tradeoff documented in `authStore.ts` itself and in `BUILT.md`, backed by real evidence (zero `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` repo-wide, B-118's short access-token TTL bounds exposure), not asserted. Redirect-back-after-login (AC2) achieved without touching `AppShell.tsx`/`router.tsx` (both out of scope) via a module-load-time capture of `window.location` in `authStore.ts`, consumed once by `LoginPage.tsx`. **A planned pre-emptive JWT-expiry check on rehydration was designed, approved, then removed after live verification found it was built on a wrong assumption:** `eami-api`'s `refresh_token` is a cryptographically random opaque value (confirmed both live via `/v1/auth/login` and in `auth.go` source), not a JWT — the planned decode would have thrown and forced a logout on every hard reload, the exact bug being fixed. Removed; the existing, unmodified `lib/auth.ts` 401→refresh→`logout()` flow is relied on instead, since only the server can actually know a refresh token is dead. Full detail, including the live-verification methodology (real backend, real tokens, the actual shipped `authStore.ts` executed via an esbuild bundle under three scenarios, 11/11 assertions passing), in `BUILT.md`'s `eami-ui` section.
**Dependencies:** none. Touched only `authStore.ts`/`LoginPage.tsx`, per scope.
**Status:** DONE, 2026-09-02.

### B-132 — EPIC: Horizontal scale readiness — **logged, investigation not started, deferred/low urgency**
**Objective:** the current architecture (ADR-020 Model A) correctly assumes single-instance/vertical scaling, which is the right choice for the vast majority of realistic single-enterprise loads — **this is a deliberate, correct design decision, not a gap**, and this epic should NOT be built until a real customer approaches a genuine vertical ceiling. Logged now purely so the known single-instance assumptions are on record before that day arrives, not as a signal to start building.
**Three known single-instance assumptions that must be investigated before any horizontal scaling is assumed safe:** (a) B-070's rate limiter is in-memory/per-process — running two gateway instances would silently double the effective rate limit with no error or warning; (b) **B-043 (already logged, open)** — JWT revocation lists don't actually broadcast across instances despite claiming to, so a revoked token could still validate on a second instance; (c) **UNCHECKED, new to this entry** — does B-100's `sync.Once`-based approval-hold mechanism's in-memory pending-approval state have the same single-instance assumption as (a) and (b)? Not yet investigated; a second instance could plausibly either double-dispatch or silently strand a hold, depending on how the mechanism actually behaves under a second process — needs real tracing through `eami-gateway`'s approval package before any conclusion is drawn.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is a scoping placeholder. First step for whoever picks this up is investigating (c) above (the only genuinely unchecked item of the three) and confirming (a)/(b)'s existing severity assessments still hold, before any brief is written.
**Dependencies:** B-070, B-043, B-100 (all investigate-not-modify at this stage). Blocks nothing — explicitly not to be built until a real customer need materializes.
**Status:** logged 2026-08-29 from a scalability/DR/integrations discussion. Zero investigation started.

### B-133 — Data lifecycle/retention strategy for ever-growing TimescaleDB hypertables — **logged, investigation not started**
**Objective:** `audit_log` (append-only by design — this table is never meant to shrink, per its own tamper-evident hash-chain purpose), `token_usage`, and `paste_events` all grow unbounded with no retention policy, compression, or archival strategy defined anywhere in the codebase. Needs investigation into a real retention policy (what can safely age out vs. what must never be deleted — `audit_log` likely never, the other two plausibly compactable/archivable), TimescaleDB's native compression features (columnar compression on aged chunks, already available in the `timescale/timescaledb-ha:pg16` image already in use), and connection-pooling behavior under sustained load as data volume grows.
**Why this matters now:** per explicit user framing during this discussion, likely a more realistic near-term bottleneck than compute/horizontal scaling (B-132) — storage growth and query performance on ever-larger hypertables will bite before a single instance's CPU/memory becomes the limiting factor for a typical single-enterprise deployment.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — investigation-first item. First step is quantifying real growth rates per table (rows/day under realistic load) and confirming which tables TimescaleDB compression is actually safe to enable on without disturbing `audit_log`'s hash-chain read path.
**Dependencies:** none blocking investigation start.
**Status:** logged 2026-08-29 from a scalability/DR/integrations discussion. Zero investigation started.

### B-134 — Offsite backup + re-verification of B-029's restore test against current schema — **investigation complete, 2026-09-01, split into B-143 (fix) + B-144 (offsite design)**
**Objective (original):** B-029 (DONE 2026-07-25) built and live-verified real Postgres backup/restore — but LOCAL-VOLUME ONLY, and its original restore proof predated roughly a dozen subsequent `schema/migrations-v2` migrations. This entry investigated both halves before any design/build work.
**Part A — backup coverage re-verified, mechanism confirmed sound:** `backup-db.sh` runs a full-database `pg_dump -Fc` (no table allowlist), so it automatically captures every table added since B-029 — `ai_token_events` (migration 000010), `agent_lifecycle_events` (000009), all workflow tables (000006/000007), `token_usage`'s hypertable — with zero maintenance burden as new migrations land. No coverage gap found. One doc-accuracy gap found and folded into B-143 below: `RECOVERY.md` still claims `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/schema.sql` auto-applies on a fresh volume; confirmed empirically false since B-051 moved schema ownership to the `migrate` service (`schema/migrations-v2`) — a truly fresh `postgres_data` volume today has zero tables until `migrate` runs.
**Part B — real fresh restore test executed (not a re-read of B-029's old test):** took a live backup of the real running dev stack (non-destructive), restored it into a fully isolated `docker compose -p b134dr` stack (separate volumes/network, torn down after, zero impact to the real stack — confirmed via `docker volume ls` and a full `diff` of the restored vs. live `audit_log` table showing 0 differences). **Hash chain: 0 bad hashes across all 1636 rows**, independently recomputed via a fresh standalone verifier matching `writer.go`'s exact formula (not reused test code) — restore preserves tamper-evidence integrity perfectly. (205/1636 "broken link" rows found in *both* the restored copy and the untouched live DB — proven pre-existing via a byte-identical `diff`, not restore-caused; consistent with B-140's already-tracked concurrent-writer race on the global unscoped `GetLastHash()`, not this entry's to fix.) Workflow/approval/credential-store row counts (`workflows`, `workflow_steps`, `approval_requests`, `gateway_tools.credentials_encrypted`) all matched live exactly; all 3 real encrypted credential blobs decrypted successfully with the real key post-restore.
**Real restore bug found — logged fresh as B-143:** `pg_restore` (even via the already-`--clean`-avoiding documented procedure) fails to apply 4 FK constraints on hypertables (`gateway_node_metrics_node_id_fkey`, `paste_events_org_id_fkey`, `paste_events_source_endpoint_id_fkey`) with `ERROR: ONLY option not supported on hypertable operations` — the same bug *family* B-029 originally found on `--clean`'s `DROP CONSTRAINT`, now hit on plain `ADD CONSTRAINT` too. Row data is unaffected (COPY precedes the failing ALTERs), but referential-integrity enforcement on those two tables is silently absent post-restore. Confirmed a working fix by hand (dropping `ONLY` from the `ALTER TABLE` succeeds).
**Part C — offsite mechanism recommendation, not built — logged fresh as B-144:** grounded in ADR-020 Model A ("zero data ever reaches EAMI-operated infrastructure"), ruling out any EAMI-operated cloud target. Recommended: opt-in customer-configured remote target (S3-compatible/SFTP via `rclone`/`restic`), disabled by default, with the dump encrypted (age/GPG, customer-supplied key) before it ever leaves the host — RECOVERY.md's own "Future work" note on backup encryption was correct and is now load-bearing, not deferrable further.
**Part D — scope:** re-verification (this entry) was small and is now done. The restore fix (B-143) is small, contained, immediately buildable. The offsite mechanism (B-144) is medium — config surface, encryption, transport, a real tested remote-restore drill — correctly kept as its own brief rather than bundled, so B-143's quick fix doesn't wait on B-144's design work.
**Dependencies:** B-029 (extends). **Status:** investigation complete 2026-09-01, no code changes made, per explicit user direction (investigation-only brief). Full drill detail in `CONTEXT.md`'s 2026-09-01 entry.

### B-135 — Observability/SIEM integration exports — **logged, investigation not started**
**Objective:** two distinct capabilities, explicitly to be scoped separately when investigated, not built as one brief: **(a) metrics export** — a Prometheus-compatible (or similar) endpoint so a customer's own monitoring stack can watch EAMI's resource usage/health directly; this also directly serves real-world scale-timing decisions (i.e., feeds the "has a customer actually hit a vertical ceiling" question B-132 says must be answered before horizontal scaling is built). **(b) audit-log export to a customer's existing SIEM** (Splunk, Microsoft Sentinel, etc.) — a natural enterprise expectation for a product whose core value proposition is a tamper-evident audit trail; customers governed by compliance requirements will expect to feed that trail into infrastructure they already run, not just view it in `eami-ui`.
**Explicitly distinct from B-137 ("Third-party public API")** — customers building integrations against EAMI's own platform; this entry is the reverse direction, EAMI feeding data out into a customer's existing tools. **Resolution of this entry's original flag:** at the time this entry was first written, that epic could not be located anywhere in `BACKLOG.md`/`CONTEXT.md`/`ROADMAP.md` under that name; confirmed 2026-08-29 it genuinely had never been committed to the repo (existed only in prior conversation and external reference documents) and has now been logged properly as **B-137**.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet. First step is scoping (a) and (b) as two independent investigations/briefs, per the objective above.
**Dependencies:** (a) has a soft dependency on B-132 (informs, doesn't block, the scale-timing decision). (b) depends on the existing audit-log hash-chain/export surface (`audit.Writer`, B-078/093) as its data source.
**Status:** logged 2026-08-29 from a scalability/DR/integrations discussion. Zero investigation started. **Superseded in part, 2026-08-29: sub-item (a) (metrics export) is replaced/refined by the more specific B-136 half 1** (real named metrics — dispatch latency, error/timeout rate, cost burn rate, approval queue depth, policy hot-reload health — rather than this entry's generic "resource usage/health"). **Sub-item (b) (audit-log export to a customer's SIEM) is NOT covered by B-136 and remains open here, unchanged** — B-136 does not mention SIEM audit-log export at all; its own scope is EAMI-native metrics packaging plus ingestion FROM third-party observability tools, a different direction from pushing the audit trail out to Splunk/Sentinel. Whoever picks up (b) should treat this entry as the live one for that half.

### B-136 — EPIC: Critical operational metrics + third-party observability ingestion — **logged, investigation not started**
**Objective:** two distinct, complementary halves, to be scoped and investigated separately, not built as one brief.
**Half 1 — EAMI-native metrics export (smaller, extends existing data, not new analysis capability):** expose real operational metrics EAMI already touches, in a standard consumable format (e.g. a Prometheus-style endpoint) — dispatch latency per connector/model, error/timeout rate per connector, real-time cost burn rate (building directly on B-097-112's already-verified pricing accuracy, not recomputing it), approval queue depth/time-to-resolution, and policy hot-reload health (directly relevant given **B-129's own critical find** that hot-reload failure was previously silent — a health metric here would have surfaced that class of bug operationally, not just via manual investigation). This refines/replaces B-135(a)'s generic "resource usage/health" framing with these specific, real metrics.
**Half 2 — ingestion FROM third-party observability/LLM-quality tools (larger, genuinely new direction, not yet attempted anywhere in this codebase):** rather than building EAMI's own model-behavior/reasoning-trace/drift-detection capability — a mature, separate discipline already well-served by dedicated tools like Traceloop/LangSmith, and **explicitly NOT a category EAMI should compete in directly**, per the existing ServiceNow battle card's own honest gap disclosure — investigate accepting signals FROM those tools into EAMI's governance layer instead. **Real open design question, requiring investigation before any build:** should an ingested signal (e.g. a detected drift/bias flag from a customer's own Traceloop instance) be purely informational/displayed within EAMI, or should it be able to actually influence a policy decision or trigger an escalation? The latter — a genuinely new capability, not a display feature — would be real differentiation: EAMI as the place where governance and observability signals from wherever a customer already trusts them converge, rather than a competing observability platform. No mechanism for either informational display or decision-influencing ingestion exists today; this is a from-scratch design question, not an extension of an existing pipeline.
**Relationship to B-135:** replaces/refines B-135(a) (metrics export) with the specific metric list in Half 1 above. Does NOT touch or replace B-135(b) (audit-log export to a customer's SIEM, e.g. Splunk/Sentinel) — that remains a separate, still-open, un-superseded item; Half 2's "ingestion FROM third-party tools" here is the reverse data direction from B-135(b)'s "export audit trail TO SIEM," and the two should not be conflated.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is a scoping placeholder for both halves. First step for whoever picks this up: scope Half 1 as its own smaller brief (real metric names/endpoint shape) independent of Half 2's open design question, which needs a real product decision (informational-only vs. decision-influencing) before any code is written.
**Dependencies:** Half 1 depends on B-097-112 (pricing accuracy, for the cost-burn-rate metric) and B-129 (policy hot-reload mechanism, for the reload-health metric) as read-only data sources — extends, does not modify, either. Half 2 has no code dependencies yet; its dependency is a product/design decision, not a technical one.
**Status:** logged 2026-08-29, idea discussed, not investigated. Confirmed B-ID free (counter stood at B-136, no collision) before minting.

Now logged properly as **B-138** (IdP/SSO) and **B-139** (agentless discovery) — as of 2026-08-29, all four of the original strategic epics are durably recorded in the repo.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is a scoping placeholder. First step for whoever picks this up is deciding what data/control surface the API exposes in v1 (read-only governance data first is the obvious lower-risk starting scope, vs. write access to policies/approvals) and whether `api_keys` is reused or a new credential type is needed.
**Dependencies:** `api_keys` (B-098) as the likely credential foundation — extends, does not modify, unless investigation finds it unsuitable.

### B-140 — `TestDispatch_Escalate_Resolution_Expired_WritesSecondRow` has now failed three times in CI with an identical hash-chain-reset signature — **investigation complete, confirmed test-infrastructure-only, real fix identified, fix priority bumped after a third recurrence**
**Objective:** investigate the actual race mechanism behind a recurring CI failure in `eami-gateway/cmd/gateway/dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go:310`. **Occurrence 1** (CI run 33202211503, job 98954194411, commit `f510f17`, 2026-08-28): failed with `rows[0]` claiming a `prev_hash` matching no real row and not the genesis hash — "the chain was reset, not continued" — immediately preceded by a WARN showing a severed TLS/SASL DB connection mid-test. **Occurrence 2** (CI run 33235262263, job 99055028435, commit `15c0e9d`, 2026-08-29): same test, same file/line, **identical failure signature** ("the chain was reset, not continued"), but this time with no visible connection issue beforehand — instead an `episodes_org_id_fkey` violation WARN appeared immediately *after* the failure. **The pattern itself is the finding:** the same symptom surfacing via two different proximate triggers (a dropped connection once, an apparent org deletion the other time) is more consistent with a real, timing-sensitive race in shared test state (`audit_log`/`orgs`) than with either trigger being independently causal.
**Investigated already, not skipped:** re-ran the full `eami-gateway` suite against a fresh, CI-matching Postgres (`timescale/timescaledb-ha:pg16`, real `migrate` CLI schema apply) after occurrence 1 — 3 full `go test ./... -count=1` runs (all green) plus 20 targeted repetitions of this specific test and its 3 siblings (20/20 pass), zero reproduction. The clean local reproduction, combined with two real CI failures with an identical signature, points at CI's own runner conditions (shared/throttled compute, different goroutine-scheduling jitter under contention than a quiet local Docker Desktop machine) surfacing a race window that doesn't open locally — not at the underlying mechanism being imaginary.
**Related, contributing hypothesis — not the same item:** **B-122** already documents that `audit_log` has no FK to `orgs` and no cleanup mechanism, so concurrently-running test packages within one `go test ./...` invocation (all packages share one real Postgres service container per CI job) can plausibly race against each other's `t.Cleanup`-based `DELETE FROM orgs` / INSERT sequences. That's a real candidate mechanism for occurrence 2's FK violation, but B-122's entry is about the *permanent orphaned-row accumulation* problem specifically — this entry is about the *specific race* that can make `TestDispatch_Escalate_Resolution_Expired_WritesSecondRow`'s own hash-chain-continuity assertion fail outright, which is a distinct, more acute question: does this expose a real gap in the resolution-audit-write path itself (B-124/B-125, adversarially verified but not against concurrent-package interference), or is it purely a test-infrastructure isolation gap with zero production analogue? That distinction is exactly what needs investigating, not assumed either way.
**Explicitly NOT fixed here — investigation only, same discipline as B-100's original TOCTOU investigation:** do not reflexively patch around the symptom (e.g., widening a timeout, retrying the assertion) before the actual race is understood. First steps for whoever picks this up: (1) determine whether `cmd/gateway`'s tests and `internal/workflow`'s tests (or any other package) can genuinely run concurrently against the same Postgres connection/service container within one CI job's `go test ./...` invocation, and if so, trace a concrete interleaving that produces both observed symptoms; (2) determine whether this test's unusually tight `hold_timeout=300ms` (visible in both occurrences' logs) makes it uniquely vulnerable to CI scheduling jitter independent of any cross-package DB race, as an alternative or contributing explanation; (3) only then decide whether the fix belongs in test isolation (B-122's domain) or in the production resolution-audit-write path itself (B-124/B-125's domain).
**Severity/Priority:** Medium-High — per explicit user direction, elevated above B-122's existing Medium-Low because this is a second, identical-signature recurrence (not a single data point) and it touches the same escalation-resolution audit-write path B-124/B-125 built and adversarially verified. Worth taking seriously as a possible real correctness question, not routed around as "just CI flakiness."
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this entry is the investigation record. First step is the three-part investigation above, before any fix (test-isolation or production-code) is proposed.
**Dependencies:** B-122 (contributing hypothesis, shared `audit_log`/`orgs` test-isolation gap — related but distinct scope). B-124/B-125 (the production resolution-audit-write path this test actually exercises — investigate whether it's implicated, don't assume either way).
**Status:** logged 2026-08-29 following a second CI recurrence with an identical failure signature. B-ID confirmed free (counter stood at B-140, no collision) before minting.

**Investigation conclusion (2026-08-29): central question resolved with code-level proof, not inference — this is a test-infrastructure race, confirmed structurally impossible in production, not merely unlikely.**
**Mechanism, confirmed by direct code reading:** `internal/audit/writer.go`'s `Writer.Write()` is internally race-free — a single `Writer` instance correctly chains its own writes via an in-memory, mutex-protected `lastHash` (`writer.go:107-169`), which is exactly what B-124/125's own `TestWriter_RealDB_HashChainRemainsValid_WithWorkflowLinkage`/`...WithDataHandling` already proved. The one unguarded moment is `Writer`'s **lazy first-write initialization**: `GetLastHash()` runs `SELECT hash FROM audit_log ORDER BY timestamp DESC LIMIT 1` with **no org scoping at all** (`writer.go:178-190`) — a genuinely global query, by design (the hash chain is one process-wide sequence across all orgs, confirmed via `writer_pg_test.go`'s own comment). Every `cmd/gateway` dispatcher test constructs a **fresh** `Writer` (`dispatcher_test.go:109,124`, `audit.NewWriter` called per-test, not shared/long-lived as in production `main.go:86`), so each test's first write genuinely re-queries the live global tip at that moment. Separately, `internal/audit/writer_pg_test.go` has 5 real-Postgres tests that each construct their own fresh `Writer` AND register `t.Cleanup` calling `deleteAuditRow` — a real, targeted `DELETE FROM audit_log WHERE id = $1` (`writer_pg_test.go:81-91`) for every row they insert. **CI runs `go test ./... -count=1 -v` with no `-p` flag anywhere in the repo** (`.github/workflows/build.yml:83`, confirmed via direct search), so Go's default cross-package test parallelism means `internal/audit` and `cmd/gateway` genuinely run concurrently against the one shared Postgres service container per CI job. If an `internal/audit` test's row happens to be the global tip when `cmd/gateway`'s dispatcher test initializes its `Writer`, and `internal/audit`'s own `t.Cleanup` deletes that row before `cmd/gateway`'s `verifyHashChain()` runs, the existence check at `dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go:165-175` correctly reports the predecessor gone — exactly "the chain was reset, not continued."
**Not a new discovery — this exact race class was already found once and partially mitigated by the original B-124/125 session itself:** `dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go:139-158`'s own comment states an earlier draft snapshotted the chain head externally and "failed intermittently under `go test ./...`'s cross-package parallelism," was changed to trust `rows[0]`'s own stored value, and a code-review pass added the current existence-check as a guard against a genuinely-reset chain — but that guard has a residual window it can't close: it can't distinguish "genuinely reset" from "genuinely existed, then legitimately deleted by an unrelated package's own cleanup."
**Confirmed structurally impossible in production, not just unlikely:** `audit_log` is real, RLS-enforced INSERT-only for the app role in production (`schema/migrations-v2/000001`'s own REVOKE UPDATE/DELETE, referenced directly in `writer_pg_test.go:83-89`). Production has no DELETE path into this table at all, and `main.go:86` constructs exactly one long-lived `Writer` for the entire gateway process lifetime — so the one vulnerable moment (initial `GetLastHash()`) happens once per process boot, with no concurrent DELETE-capable actor ever present outside tests. **B-124/B-125's resolution-audit-write path itself is exonerated** — this is not a gap in that work.
**Why local reproduction (3 full runs + 20 targeted reruns, zero failures) stayed clean despite identical structural exposure:** local and CI runs use the identical command shape (no `-p` override either place), so the race opportunity is structurally the same. The confirmed, measured difference is raw scheduling headroom: local machine has 20 logical CPUs (`nproc`, verified) vs. GitHub-hosted `ubuntu-latest` runners' small, shared, frequently CPU-throttled VMs — a fast, lightly-loaded local machine gives every goroutine a short, predictable path from query to result, while a noisier CI runner stretches and jitters that path, plausibly opening this race's narrow window more often. Presented as the best-supported explanation available, not asserted as certain.
**Confirmed systemic, not local to this one test file:** the vulnerable pattern (fresh `audit.Writer` + unscoped `GetLastHash()` + real per-row test-cleanup deletes) is used identically across `internal/audit/writer_pg_test.go`'s 5 tests and every `cmd/gateway` dispatcher test. Any real-Postgres test in `eami-gateway` that reads back "the chain" rather than only its own row is symmetrically exposed — this test just happens to be the one that explicitly asserts on chain continuity, so it's the one that visibly fails.
**Real fix identified, resolving B-122's open fork:** adopt `eami-api/internal/api/bootstrap_test.go`'s already-proven per-test throwaway-database pattern (`CREATE DATABASE`/apply real migrations/`DROP DATABASE`, mirroring `schema/migrationtest`'s own precedent) for `eami-gateway`'s real-Postgres tests. This closes this race category **and** B-122's original orphaned-row accumulation concern in one motion — full isolation means no cross-package interference is possible at all, and nothing is left behind to orphan. This resolves B-122's "isolated test DB vs. accept-and-document" fork in favor of isolation, now backed by concrete evidence rather than a cost/benefit guess. See B-122's updated entry.
**Severity/Priority, revised post-investigation:** the *investigation* was correctly Medium-High (a second identical-signature recurrence touching an adversarially-verified production path deserved real scrutiny, not dismissal). The *actual fix* is lower urgency now that production is provably unaffected — this becomes a real, contained follow-up brief (adopt throwaway-DB isolation for `eami-gateway` tests) whenever picked up, not an urgent item, but no longer purely theoretical either: it has two confirmed real CI occurrences and a fully traced mechanism behind it.
**Acceptance criteria for the follow-up fix brief, once picked up:** `eami-gateway`'s real-Postgres test packages (`internal/audit`, `cmd/gateway`, `internal/workflow`, others as inventoried) each run against their own throwaway database via `bootstrap_test.go`'s pattern; `TestDispatch_Escalate_Resolution_Expired_WritesSecondRow` and its siblings pass with zero cross-package interference possible by construction; B-122's orphaned-row concern closes as a side effect, not a separate fix.
**Dependencies:** B-122 (this investigation resolves B-122's own open design fork — see its updated entry). B-124/B-125 (exonerated, not implicated — investigated and confirmed, not assumed).
**Status:** **investigation complete, 2026-08-29.** Central question resolved: confirmed test-infrastructure-only, confirmed structurally impossible in production. No code changed — per explicit user direction, this investigation stayed investigation-only, matching B-100's own precedent. The identified fix (throwaway-DB isolation, per `bootstrap_test.go`'s pattern) is a real, contained, not-yet-started follow-up brief whenever picked up.

**Occurrence 3 (2026-09-01) — real CI recurrence, priority bumped.** CI run `33451297084`, job `99681557773` ("Test — eami-gateway"), commit `3873f9b` (B-128's org-scoping fix — a real code change, not a docs-only commit like occurrence 1). Same test, same file/line (`dispatcher_escalation_resolution_test.go:310` — confirmed by direct source read: the literal `t.Fatalf` lives at line 174 inside `verifyHashChain`, which calls `t.Helper()` at line 160, so Go correctly attributes the failure to line 310, `verifyHashChain`'s call site inside the test itself), byte-for-byte identical signature (`decision="escalated"`, prev_hash matching no real row and not genesis, "the chain was reset, not continued"). **Verified against the real raw log text, not the GitHub "Explain error" summary panel** (which has been wrong 3 times running tonight on unrelated generic FK/UUID stories) **and not assumed from the pattern alone** — the log was pasted directly into the conversation (browser-obtained, after the GitHub API's log-download endpoint returned a 403 requiring authenticated admin rights even on this public repo) and cross-checked line-for-line against the actual current source before logging. **Three identical-signature recurrences in one session is real signal, per explicit user direction: this moves the already-identified throwaway-DB-isolation fix up the priority queue** — still not Critical/Urgent (the production-impossibility analysis above is unchanged and unaffected by a third test-only occurrence), but no longer a "whenever picked up, no rush" item either. **Corroborating evidence for B-128, partially confirmed only:** the same log excerpt shows `TestDispatch_OrgScoping_TwoOrgsConflictingPolicies` starting cleanly (real policy load, no crash) immediately after this failure, consistent with B-128's fix being unaffected by this unrelated pre-existing race — but the pasted excerpt cuts off before that test's own `--- PASS`/`--- FAIL` line, and `TestLoad_OrgScoping_CrossOrgDispatchNoLongerMatches` (a different package) doesn't appear in it at all, so this is *not* an independently re-verified PASS for either — flagged as such rather than overclaimed. Full go test run made locally against real Postgres (see B-128's own BUILT.md entry) remains the actual verified-green evidence for both.
**Dependencies:** unchanged. **Priority:** Medium-High investigation (unchanged) → **fix now recommended as a near-term follow-up, not deferred**, per this third recurrence.

### B-142 — `registry.go`'s `LookupByNameAndOrg` doc comment still asserts the disproven "safe by construction" reasoning that produced B-141 — **Low, logged not fixed, found independently by both of B-141's mandatory review passes**
**Objective:** `eami-gateway/internal/registry/registry.go` lines 104-108 (in `LookupByNameAndOrg`'s own doc comment) still reads: *"`LookupByName`'s unscoped `WHERE name = $1 LIMIT 1` is safe for every existing caller (`internal/mcp`, `internal/episode`) because their name always comes from a signature-verified JWT sub, itself scoped to whichever single org issued that token."* This is now factually wrong on both counts, confirmed by both B-141 review passes independently: (a) neither `internal/mcp` nor `internal/episode` calls `LookupByName` any more — both were migrated to `LookupByNameAndOrg` by B-141 — and (b) this is verbatim the exact reasoning that produced the real, traced B-141 cross-tenant episode-content leak; it was never actually sound (a JWT `sub` being signed doesn't make the *name* org-unique, since `gateway_agents` only enforces `UNIQUE(org_id, name)`).
**Why this is worth fixing, not just cosmetic:** a repo-wide grep confirms `LookupByName` now has zero remaining production callers — it and its name-only 30s cache are dead code, kept alive only by this comment's stale justification. Left as-is, a future engineer adding a new agent-identity consumption call site could read this comment, see `internal/mcp`/`internal/episode` cited as intentional, safe precedent for the unscoped query, and reintroduce the exact bug B-141 just closed.
**Fix sketch:** either (a) correct the doc comment to reflect that `LookupByName` is no longer safe for any real caller and should not be used for new code, or (b) delete `LookupByName` and its name-only cache entirely now that nothing calls it, and update the comment accordingly. Small, contained, no design decision needed.
**Severity:** Low — no functional/security impact today (the method is unused), pure landmine-prevention for future code.
**Dependencies:** B-141 (the fix that made this comment stale). **Status:** logged only, not fixed — out of B-141's own MAY MODIFY scope (`registry.go` was read-only for that brief), disclosed per this session's standing practice.

### B-143 — Restore fails to apply 4 hypertable FK constraints (`gateway_node_metrics`, `paste_events`); `RECOVERY.md`'s initdb claim is stale — **DONE, 2026-09-01**
**Objective:** A real, fresh restore drill against the current schema (B-134, 2026-09-01) found that `pg_restore`, run via `RECOVERY.md`'s own already-`--clean`-avoiding documented procedure, still fails to apply `gateway_node_metrics_node_id_fkey`, `paste_events_org_id_fkey`, and `paste_events_source_endpoint_id_fkey` with `ERROR: ONLY option not supported on hypertable operations` — the same bug family as B-029's original `--clean`/`DROP CONSTRAINT` finding, now hit on `ADD CONSTRAINT` instead. Confirmed against a live comparison DB that these constraints exist there and are genuinely absent post-restore, not merely renamed/relocated.
**Why now, not routine:** row data is unaffected, but any inserts into `gateway_node_metrics`/`paste_events` after a real production restore would go unprotected by their FK until this is fixed — a real, silent referential-integrity gap in the one disaster-recovery path the product has.
**Second, bundled fix:** `RECOVERY.md`'s restore procedure still asserts `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/schema.sql` auto-applies on a fresh volume — confirmed empirically false since B-051 moved schema ownership to the `migrate` service; a genuinely fresh `postgres_data` volume today has zero tables until `migrate` runs. Doesn't break the restore (DROP SCHEMA CASCADE on an empty schema is a harmless no-op) but the documented reasoning misleads an operator trying to understand what's happening.
**Resolution:** new `scripts/restore-db.sh` wraps the documented schema-wipe + `pg_restore` steps and automatically repairs this exact failure class — deliberately generic (parses `pg_restore`'s stderr for the "hypertable" error text paired with a failed `ALTER TABLE ONLY ... ADD CONSTRAINT`, strips `ONLY`, re-applies), not a hardcoded list of today's 2 tables, so a future hypertable-FK-bearing table is covered automatically too. **Fails loudly by design:** any `pg_restore` error that doesn't match that exact shape is left alone and reported, script exits non-zero rather than guessing — verified against a synthetic fixture mixing 1 real hypertable error with 2 unrelated ones, correctly repairing only the 1 and flagging the other 2. `docker-compose.yml`/`docker-compose.prod.yml` updated to mount the new script into `eami-backup`, matching `backup-db.sh`'s existing pattern; `.gitattributes` updated with the same `eol=lf` pinning `backup-db.sh` already has (same bind-mount-into-Alpine situation). `RECOVERY.md`'s stale initdb claim corrected — `pg_restore` recreates the full schema (including extensions) from the dump directly, so `migrate` running first isn't actually required for restore.
**Acceptance criteria:**
- [x] A real restore drill (fresh isolated `docker compose -p b143dr` stack, real backup, real restore, run exactly as newly documented via `eami-backup`) completes successfully — `pg_restore` itself still reports the 4 known errors internally (unavoidable given how it always generates `ALTER TABLE ONLY`), but `restore-db.sh` detects, repairs, and verifies all 4 automatically before reporting success; the *procedure* an operator runs ends in a clean, verified state, not a manual "check for zero errors" step
- [x] `pg_constraint` on the restored instance matches the live source instance exactly for `gateway_node_metrics` and `paste_events` (0-line `diff` of both constraint sets)
- [x] `RECOVERY.md` describes the current (`migrate`-service-owned) schema-provisioning path accurately
- [x] No regression: full B-134 checklist re-run and confirmed clean (hash chain 0 bad hashes/1636 rows, workflow/approval/credential-store counts identical to live, all 3 real credentials still decrypt)
**Dependencies:** B-029 (extends), B-134 (found it). **Status:** logged 2026-09-01, not fixed — investigation-only brief, per explicit user direction.

### B-144 — Offsite/off-host backup storage for the on-prem appliance (Model A) — **DONE, 2026-09-01**
**Objective:** B-029's backup mechanism is local-volume-only — a lost or compromised host loses its backups too, not just live data. This is the offsite-storage half of the original B-134 item, split out per explicit user direction so it didn't block B-143's small, immediately-buildable fix.
**Design, grounded in ADR-020 Model A** ("zero data ever reaches EAMI-operated infrastructure," one appliance per customer): rules out any EAMI-operated cloud destination by design. **Shipped:** an opt-in, customer-configured remote target (S3-compatible or SFTP via `rclone`), disabled by default — no separate flag, `scripts/backup-offsite.sh` structurally no-ops unless `OFFSITE_AGE_RECIPIENT`/`OFFSITE_RCLONE_REMOTE`/a real `secrets/rclone.conf` are all present. `rclone` chosen over `restic`: `restic` owns its own repository format and encryption, which would conflict with the customer-supplied-key requirement; `rclone` is a stateless single-purpose transport tool with no competing encryption. `age` chosen over GPG for the same "small, direct tool" reasoning.
**Real security consideration, resolved not just flagged:** `pg_dump` archives contain `audit_log`'s full content and tool-call `parameters` (JSONB) in the clear — the offsite copy is protected by encrypting the *entire* dump file with `age` (customer-supplied public key) before any network request, so every byte becomes opaque ciphertext regardless of which application-layer fields were or weren't already encrypted; empirically confirmed via `strings`/`pg_restore -f -` (424 and 3 plaintext occurrences respectively in the raw dump, 0 in the `.age` file — see `BUILT.md`).
**Acceptance criteria:**
- [x] Feature disabled by default — local backup behavior byte-for-byte unchanged (confirmed live: clean `backup-offsite: ... skipping` log line, identical local-only backup)
- [x] Enabled + configured: produces an `age`-encrypted file undecryptable without the customer's private key (confirmed: fails with no identity and with a well-formed wrong identity, succeeds with the correct one)
- [x] Encrypted file transported to a real test destination (real MinIO container, genuine S3 API) using only customer-supplied credentials (confirmed via `mc ls` against the real bucket)
- [x] Full offsite restore drill (download, decrypt, restore via the unmodified B-143 `restore-db.sh`) succeeds and passes the full B-134/B-143 verification checklist (hash chain 0 bad hashes/1636 rows, workflow/approval/credential-store counts identical to live, all 3 real credentials decrypt) — run from a second, completely separate isolated stack simulating total original-host loss
- [x] JSONB/audit-content protection question (AC5) resolved explicitly: whole-file encryption is sufficient, confirmed empirically, not left ambiguous
**Dependencies:** B-029 (extends), B-134 (source investigation), B-143 (DONE — the restore procedure this builds on top of).
**Status:** DONE, 2026-09-01. Full detail in `BUILT.md`'s Cross-cutting section.

### B-158 — Curated one-click connector/MCP registry — **logged, not started**
**Objective:** let an admin add a real, popular integration (GitHub, Slack, Salesforce, Atlassian, etc.) to `gateway_tools` with one click, instead of today's only two paths: hand-typing a `rest_api` connector's `base_url`/`action_paths` manually (B-046), or pasting/uploading a full OpenAPI spec for auto-discovery (B-075). Both existing paths still require the admin to already know or find the target service's own API shape; a curated registry ships that knowledge pre-baked for a known set of popular services.
**Source:** direct, hands-on UX research against TrueFoundry's actual live trial instance (not marketing material) — confirmed as a real, shipped, validated pattern in a direct competitor's product, not a speculative feature idea.
**Why this matters:** setup friction is a real adoption barrier for exactly the connectors most customers will want first (GitHub/Slack/Salesforce/Atlassian are default-common, not edge cases) — B-046/B-075 both still put the full burden of knowing the target API's shape on the admin. A curated registry trades that burden for a small, EAMI-maintained set of pre-built definitions.
**Likely shape, not yet designed:** a new admin-facing picker (reusing the existing Add Tool panel per the UI-consistency mandate — `DataTable`/B-104, detail-panel convention) listing a curated set of known services; selecting one pre-fills `base_url`/`action_paths`/credential-field shape, leaving only the customer's own credentials to supply. Whether entries are static (shipped with EAMI, updated on release cadence) or something more dynamic is an open question for whoever scopes this — not decided here.
**Explicitly not investigated yet:** which services to curate first, how entries are authored/maintained/versioned, how this interacts with B-075's OpenAPI-discovery path (a curated entry is plausibly just a pre-supplied OpenAPI spec plus credential metadata, worth checking before assuming a separate mechanism is needed), and whether/how a customer could contribute or override a curated definition.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this is a logged feature idea, not a scoped brief.
**Dependencies:** B-044/B-046 (manual `rest_api` connector config, the baseline this improves on), B-075 (OpenAPI auto-discovery, likely the underlying mechanism a curated entry pre-supplies), B-104 (`DataTable`, reuse per UI-consistency mandate).
**Severity/Priority:** Medium — real, validated UX improvement, not urgent; genuine competitive gap but not blocking any existing customer path.
**Status:** logged 2026-09-03, sourced from direct competitor UX research. B-ID confirmed free before minting (counter stood at B-158, no collision). Zero investigation or code started.

### B-159 — Hosted STDIO-to-HTTP MCP server wrapping — **logged, not started**
**Objective:** let a customer host a local/stdio-based MCP server as a real, network-accessible streamable-HTTP MCP server, so stdio-only MCP servers (a common shape for community/local MCP tooling) become usable as real Gateway-routed tools without the customer having to build their own HTTP wrapper. EAMI currently has no equivalent of this — `eami-gateway`'s own MCP surface (B-061/B-075) is a server exposing tools outward, not a mechanism for wrapping an inbound stdio process.
**Source:** direct, hands-on UX research against TrueFoundry's actual live trial instance (not marketing material) — confirmed as a real, shipped feature in a direct competitor's product.
**Priority note:** explicitly smaller and lower priority than B-158 — a real gap, but a narrower use case (customers running local/stdio MCP servers they want gatewayed) than B-158's broad, default-common connector-setup friction.
**Explicitly not investigated yet:** where this would run (a per-customer on-prem process the appliance manages, given ADR-020's Model A constraint against any EAMI-operated hosting of customer processes — a live stdio subprocess is a materially different trust surface than the existing `mcp`-type `gateway_tools` row, which today has no working direct-dispatch path per B-061's own scope note), how process lifecycle/crash-recovery/resource limits would be handled, and how the wrapped server's tools would surface through the existing `tools/list` mechanism (B-061) once running.
**Acceptance criteria:** none yet — this is a logged feature idea, not a scoped brief.
**Dependencies:** B-061 (existing `tools/list`/MCP surface, the discovery mechanism a wrapped server's tools would need to appear through), ADR-020 (on-prem/air-gapped deployment model — constrains where a wrapping process could actually run).
**Severity/Priority:** Low — real, validated gap, narrower use case than B-158, no urgency.
**Status:** logged 2026-09-03, sourced from direct competitor UX research. B-ID confirmed free before minting (counter stood at B-158 at the time B-158 above was minted; this is the next number, no collision). Zero investigation or code started.
